३.4 Release of funds by SHS for expenditure under NRHM
3.4.1 Weakness of procurement system

Funds are released by SHS to DHSs for meeting establishment expenses of DPMU;
disbursement of grants to its peripheral units like CHCs/PHCs, RKSs, sub centres,
procurement of medicines within the powers available to CMOs and Medical Officers
in-charge of health centres etc. Major part of funds for procurement of medicines, equipment
and construction were released to technical Directorates like DGNPME, DGMH, Director
General, Medical Education (DGME), Information, Education and Communication (IEC)
Bureau and CHART, working under the same administrative department.

3.4.2, Banking arrangements

Gol issued (December 2006) revised instructions’* to the State to streamline the banking
arrangements for a centralised system for flow of funds. Integrated Health Societies at State
and district levels were to be created to enable a single budget head for NRHM at the State
level, facilitating single window receipt, single management reporting centre, single
Utilisation Certificate (UC) to Gol and a consolidated audit for all the programmes run by
SHS and DHSs. The system also envisaged creation of a Financial Management Group to
take over the activities of processing fund releases, accounting for the expenditure reported
by subordinate units and monitoring of UCs and audit arrangements.

Further, the old accounts were to be closed with effect from March 2007 and new accounts
operated from April 2007 in the SBI group of accounts. Non-closing of old accounts was to
be considered a cognisable offence. Pictorial presentations of the fund and banking
management in the new system, at SHS and DHSs’ levels and Fund Flow Reporting process
under NRHM are given in Appendix-3.4, 3.5 and 3.6.

Despite constant pursuance, SHS did not furnish records of banking arrangements including
instructions to banks and DHSs for opening and operation of bank accounts to prevent
unauthorised transactions.

Audit observed deficiencies in banking arrangements and transactions:

7 As per Statement of Expenditure of Director General, PMS/FW.

2 PMS/EW came into existence from May 2008, under Government Order of 19 May 2008, for the purposes of fund transfer and
management. This office was renamed as DGNPME and was earlier called DGFW.

3 Gol relaxed (11 January 2007) the guidelines issued as per notification of December 2006 and allowed separate sub-accounts for
additionalities under NRHM and immunisation (for RI and Pulse Polio) both at State and district levels. All other provisions of the earlier
notification were retained.

Performance Audit Report on National Rural Health Mission for the year ended 31 March 2011 | 35


Financial Management and Accounting Control

Is No FMG group was in place with well defined responsibility centres as envisaged;

. As of March 2011, SHS had 823 bank accounts’ up to DHSs level, excluding
CHCs/PHCs/VHSCs/RKSs efc., which were not monitored centrally to control
opening, operation and utilisation of fund through these accounts;

. Unauthorised diversion of programme funds (% 7.52 crore in 18 DHSs) to non-NRHM
accounts (including private/personal accounts) was noticed. These unauthorised
accounts, although required, were not closed;

. A balance of % 5.51 crore in RCH-I was held as on 31 March 2009 in the old accounts
which was reduced to ₹ 0.83 crore as on 31 March 2010 indicating irregular transaction
of % 4.68 crore through the bank account. This was fraught with high risk of
embezzlement;

. CHC, Mauranipur, Jhansi did not close the bank account of RCH-II till March 2011.
It also did not maintain a cash book and incurred an expenditure of हैं 4.60 lakh by
issuing bearer cheques to two staff members between February and March 2011
without supporting vouchers. The likelihood of these payments being fake could not be
ruled out;

. In DHS, Kanpur Nagar, no cash book of RCH-II was maintained for 2005-06 and
2006-07 and, therefore, book balance remained indeterminable till the closure of the
accounts from April 2007. Against a balance as per bank of हैं 2.44 crore, only
% 1.76 crore was carried over in three designated bank accounts. The short carry over of
% 0.68 crore remained un-reconciled and unadjusted in the accounts. The likelihood of
% 0.68 crore being misappropriated could not be ruled out; and

. Inter-unit (SHS to DHSs, DHSs to CHCs/PHCs and to additional PHCs/sub
centres/VHSCs) reconciliation of funds had never been carried out.

These deficiencies were indicators of gross mismanagement of NRHM funds which was
fraught with very high risk of embezzlement. Lack of constant monitoring by SHS had led to
unauthorised diversion of fund and retention of fund at DHS level.

As regards control and monitoring of bank accounts, the Government stated that only the
opening of group accounts was centralised and operation and monitoring was left to the
concerned districts. In case of Mauranipur (Jhansi) it stated that irregularities were due to
shortage of staff as well as lack of awareness of accounting rules. In respect of Kanpur
Nagar, while furnishing reply, it did not furnish any justification for difference. The
Government did not furnish replies on other points.

= (2 February 2009) order’ for refund of the amount. Ex-CMO refunded & 1.06 crore

Thus, transfer of NRHM fund to a personal bank account led to misappropriation of

3.4.4. Transfer of funds through non-NRHM route

The management of NRHM funds in the State is primarily the responsibility of SHS and the
Mission Director. The institutional arrangement provided that the State created an integrated
Society (called SHS) by merging all the existing Societies. The release and utilisation of fund
was, however, characterised by diffusion of responsibility by entrusting procurement and
construction works to PMS/FW.

Audit observed that SHS transferred fund irregularly to the PMS/FW with Director General
(Family Welfare, now NPME) as its head. As informed,'” the Society which had three!®
accounts in SBI, Lucknow, was not registered but PIN and TAN nos. of the Family Welfare
Department were used for its transactions. Thus, the Society neither had any legal standing
nor was it covered under the institutional arrangements agreed to in paragraph 8.2 of MoU.
Further, some other societies also existed but information in their respect was not furnished.
Audit further observed that SPMU transferred % 36.50 crore (2009-11) to PMS/FW. The
latter, in turn, transferred (2009-11) the same to CMOs through drafts/cheques which were
not permitted under the new banking arrangement. Neither the funds nor the expenditure
thereof were captured in DHSs' accounts. Thus, हैं 36.50 crore, so transferred (details of
transfers for other years not available), remained out of DHSs’ accounts. This was violative
of Memorandum of Association of SHS, which devolves the responsibility of disbursement
of NRHM funds to implementing agencies and not to any Society, not recognised within
NRHM framework. The details of these irregular transfers are given in Appendix-3.7.

without any registration and in contravention to the directives to integrate all societies into

3.5 Missing Trails’ to link the transactions

Paragraph 4.4 of Finance and Accounts Manual of NRHM provides that all programmes’
transactions should be cross-referenced so as to link them to each item of expenditure with
budget heads, project components and expenditure categories compatible with classification
of expenditure and sources of funds.

Audit, however, observed that the prevalent system was deficient and it neither provided
sufficient assurance nor trail to the management to satisfy/ensure that the system prescribed
was being meticulously followed. Audit observed:

. Non-adherence to the prescribed system for tracking of assets, advances, bank balances
etc.;

° Absence of review of the annual accounts and annual reports by GB, as prescribed in
the Memorandum of Association;

. Absence of inter unit and intra fund reconciliation and a robust procedure to handle
cash and bank related matters;

° Absence of adequate skilled human resource to handle the finance and accounting
functions; and

. Failure to computerise the financial management and accounting functions to ensure
proper data management.

The Government stated that it had provided registers and records as maintained in SPMU.

Further, it had noted the suggestions with regard to the maintenance of accounts, review of

annual accounts, inter unit and inter fund reconciliation, financial monitoring,
computerisation of financial management and accounting system efc. for future compliance.

3.6 Control and monitoring of payments and accounting records

3.6.1 Major deficiencies/discrepancies in Accounts

Annual Accounts that provide basic data for financial monitoring and controls were marked
by deficiencies raising doubts on reliability of accounts. Some instances are discussed in
succeeding paragraphs.

3.6.2 Prescribed procedure of accounting manual not followed

The Government of Uttar Pradesh committed (22 November 2006)”° to abide by all existing
manuals, guidelines, instructions and circulars issued in connection with implementation of

“Audit trail is a sequence of steps supported by documenting the real processing of a transaction through an organisation, a process or a
system (Wikipedia from the net),
20 Paragraph 11.3 of Memorandum of Understanding between the Gol and GoUP.

38 | Performance Audit Report on National Rural Health Mission for the year ended 31 March 2011


Financial Management and Accounting Control

= programmes. To regulate proper accounting of transactions and to ensure effective
financial control, MoHFW, Government of India evolved (April 2005) the Finance and
Accounts Manual for RCH Phase II programme, which was subsequently merged
(April 2007) with NRHM programme. The manual contains finance and accounting
procedures (including formats for capturing data of expenditure) that are to be followed for
accounting of NRHM transactions.

Audit observed that SHS and DHSs did not follow the prescribed procedure. As a result,
discrepancies had arisen in the accounts, as discussed in the subsequent paragraph of the
Report.

The Statutory Auditors had also reported, that they had not come across any Finance and
Accounts Manual being followed.

The Government stated that SHS was maintaining basic records in the standard format of
double entry system. Further, the suggestion of the audit was noted for future compliance.
The reply was not correct as the cash book/ledgers were not in the prescribed format, ledgers
did not capture expenditure against advances and advance tracking register, journal book,
adjustment vouchers efc. were not maintained.

The reply was indicative of the administrative apathy not only in SHS but also in the State
Government.

3.6.3, Routing of funds through bank

The banking route for funding NRHM programmes was adopted by Gol to ensure timely
transfer of fund to the implementing agencies. The prevalent ills of operating through
treasury e.g. rush of expenditure in March, diversion of funds, delayed release of funds, non-
provision of funds (State Share), large unspent balances (lapse of surrender of fund) efc. were
still prevalent, although NRHM programmes were being executed through banks. Resorting
to such a route of funding without ensuring the safeguards of treasury system had adversely
impacted accounting controls which were compromised. Audit observed:

. Authorising and payment/cheque issuing authority were concentrated in one officer viz.
CMOs of DHSs or Medical Officer in-charges of PHCs/CHCs, who were sanctioning
and cheque drawing officers, thereby weakening the internal controls;

. Bills were not supported by sanctions and sub-vouchers;

. Monthly reconciliation of drawal from banks was not ensured to prevent fraudulent
encashment of cheques;

° Advances were disbursed without maintaining subsidiary records; and

e | NRHM funds were also transferred through unauthorised bank accounts opened
without any authority.

The Government stated that procedure with regard to flow of funds and banking process was
decided at Gol level which was adopted by all the States implementing the NRHM
programmes. However, the Government did not respond to the main issue of addressing and
improving the control mechanisms.

Performance Audit Report on National Rural Health Mission for the year ended 31 March 2011 | 39

= Non-maintenance of basic accounting records

Basic accounting records necessary to exercise proper accounting contro] were either not
maintained or not maintained in the prescribed manner. The improper maintenance/
non-maintenance of records and the likely effects on monitoring and control”! are detailed in

Audit observed:

° No journal was maintained for booking of expenditure in the ledgers against advances
which constituted major source of expenditure;

. The ledgers to record periodical expenditure were either not maintained or partially
maintained in SHS up to 2009-10 and in 22 test checked DHSs up to 2008-097;

° Expenditure of हैं 4938.74 crore,” depicted by SHS in the accounts for 2007-08 to
2010-11, was not checked as supporting journal vouchers, trial balance, consolidation
register efc., were either not maintained or not produced to Audit;

. The correctness of advances outstanding in the books of accounts (¥ 816.71 crore in
2009-10) was not checked, nor the age-wise analysis reviewed, as sub-ledgers showing
party-wise details of outstanding amounts were not available;

° The figures of expenditure shown in FMRs of PHCs and CHCs were not checked as
voucher-wise booking of expenditure under various heads of programmes was not
posted in ledgers by the units;

. Statement of Expenditures (SoEs) against advances to NGOs and others (outside the
ambit of NRHM) were accepted even when these were not supported by vouchers; and

° Expenditures against incomplete civil works were not accounted for as ‘Works—in-
Progress.’ As a result, outstanding advances against construction agencies were not
vouchsafed.

To highlight the various irregularities like non-maintenance of cash book, operation of
unauthorised bank account, advances disbursed but not captured, expenditure out of RKS
funds without authorisation of committee, unauthorised retention and utilisation of grants of
VHSC and sub-centres by CHC, apart from irregularities in procurement, implementation of
healthcare programmes efc. at the Block level, Audit findings on the functioning of CHC,
Manda, of DHS, Allahabad are presented as a Case Study in Appendix-3.9.

The Government attributed the deficiencies to internal reasons like shortage of accounting
staff and absence of proper guidelines for maintenance of accounts. The reply was not correct
as positioning of staff was the responsibility of the Government (in most cases the
expenditure was to be met by Gol) and the Accounts Manual of Gol did exist, although not
followed.

Financial Management and Accounting Control

= Major deficiencies/discrepancies in Accounts
3.8.1 Interest earned not included as receipt in the UCs

Para 12.2 of MoU stipulates that the State Government was to prepare and provide to
MoHFW a consolidated statement of expenditure, including interest that may have accrued.
Form 19 ‘A’ of GFR, in which UC along with audited statement are forwarded, also
prescribed disclosure of receipts from interest and other income, which are to form part of
unspent balance. Audit observed:

a) As per certified UCs, the unspent balance shown against different programmes
amounted to only हैं 1364.91 crore. But the actual unspent balances (comprising of
cash/bank balances and unspent advances) held on 31 March 2010 amounted to
र₹ 1420.38 crore (net after deduction of € 11.94 crore of State fund included in unspent
balance of हैं 1432.32 crore). Thus, surplus unspent balance of % 55.47 crore was short
reported in UCs to Gol. This was due to interest income wrongly shown as ‘NIL’ in
UCs;

b) PMS/FW furnishes UCs to SHS for funds advanced to it. SHS carries out adjustments
for such reported expenditure in its accounts and furnishes SoE to Gol after
incorporating figures of its accounting units. The interest of = 1.98 crore earned by
DGNPME during 2008-09 and 2009-10 on bank accounts of NRHM, was not
incorporated in SoEs sent by PMS/FW to SHS; and

c) |CHCs/PHCs had not accounted for interest earned by them on unspent balances. The
unspent balances in case of 22 test checked DHSs was % 9.71 crore at the end of
2007-08, which increased to हैं 48.67 crore (Appendix-3.10) at the end of
2009-10.

Thus, non-accountal of interest income in UCs by SHS, DGNPME and CHCs/PHCs led to
under reporting of unspent balance to Gol by हैं 57.45 crore and was violative of the
provisions of the manual.

The Government admitted under reporting of unspent balances in UCs and assured necessary
action in future.

3.8.2 Deficiencies in accounts records of SHS, PMS/FW and DHSs
3.8.2.1 Advances not captured in account

Payments in the nature of advances should be correctly captured in accounts and sub-ledgers
maintained for tracking of advances. Audit observed:

a) Ledger was not balanced and no subsidiary ledger showing unit-wise advances
outstanding was maintained by SHS up to 2009-10 in support of advances amounting to
% 816.71 crore;

b) Advances shown in Schedules to the Balance Sheet did not contain amount released to
grantees during the year and expenditure thereagainst. Consequently, difference in the
opening and closing balance of € 55.33 crore remained unexplained. Details are given
in Appendix-3.11.

Performance Audit Report on National Rural Health Mission for the year ended 31 March 2011 | 41


Financial Management and Accounting Control

= Government stated that the ledger accounts had only been partially maintained and
expenditure out of advances had directly been taken to Balance Sheet without routing
through ledger accounts due to shortage of staff and absence of proper guidelines and that the
suggestions had been noted for future compliance. The reply of the Government was
indicative of mismanaged accounting practices adopted for booking of expenditures;

c) SPMU paid हैं 1.35 lakh during February and March 2010 to DGMH against two
requests for advances but the same were charged as final expenditure in account under
Public Private Partnership expenses. Charging of advances as final expenditure without
supporting vouchers is a serious financial irregularity; and

d) Advances of % 32.81 crore to VHSCs made by PHCs/CHCs of 19 test checked districts
during 2009-10 were treated as final expenditure in Accounts which was in violation of
Para 6.18 of the Manual.

The Government stated in the exit conference and reply that the advances to VHSCs were
charged as expenditure as per provisions of Financial Guidelines and Framework of NRHM.
However, the release of subsequent advance of हैं 5000 without ensuring the utilisation of
75 per cent of the earlier advance of हैं 5000 was against the Mission Director’s circular of
31 October 2008.

3.8.2.2. Unascertainable Fixed Asset

The register for fixed assets, acquired wholly or substantially out of the Government of
India’s grants, for capital goods and non-consumable articles was to be maintained in the
prescribed format to exercise control through annual physical verification. Audit observed:

a) The fixed assets register for capital goods and non-consumable articles was not
maintained in SHS, where the aggregate value of assets held was हैं 5.92 crore as per
Accounts of 2009-10; and

b) 16 expenditure of SPMU for 2009-10 included ₹ 48.30 lakh spent on non-consumable
items like mobile phones, computers, printers etc. which were recorded in the
consumables’ stock register and the items were being shown issued periodically. The
practice of recording issue of such non-consumable items and carrying over such
reduced balances, was fraught with the risk of misuse/loss/defalcation.

The Government stated that a separate fixed assets register had now been maintained for
consumable and non-consumable items.

different schemes without any adjustment vouchers. Consequently, the position of refund of
such temporary loans could not be checked in Audit as detailed in Appendix-3.15.

The Government stated that this was due to urgency in incurring of expenditure.

3.10 Performance of Lead and Branch Auditors

The audit of SHS and DHSs is entrusted to a consortium of Chartered Accountant firms with
the Lead Auditor primarily responsible for audit of consolidated accounts of SHS and
reporting to the Management of SPMU. Adverse remarks of Branch Auditors were to be
considered by the Lead Auditor, who should disclose the manner in which he had dealt with
the comments of the Branch Auditor. However, the Lead/Branch Auditors had either not
incorporated serious observations in their reports or had inadequately disclosed without
giving the names of DHS and their financial implications. Some illustrative cases are
discussed below :
. While reviewing the bank reconciliation statements of DHS, Farrukhabad (2006-07 to
2009-10) attached to the annual accounts for 2006-07 and onwards, Audit observed that

₹ 64.87 lakh was withdrawn from Punjab National Bank (PNB) Account No. 285301
during May 2006 to April 2007 through 27 cheques, but the same were not recorded in

= cash books. Such a serious matter was not pointed out by Branch Auditors in their
teports to DHS for 2006-07 and 2009-10;

The failure of the Branch Auditors of DHS, Farrukhabad to point out the suspected
misappropriation coupled with SPMU’s failure to review the bank reconciliation
statements, led to misappropriation of ¥ 1.41 crore during 2009-10 in the same manner
by withdrawal from the bank as reported by Branch Auditor in their Report (November
2010) on the accounts for 2009-10;

Advances outstanding included suspense balances of हैं. 1.35 crore under RCH

3.14 Non-computerisation of accounts

Paragraph 6.37 of the Manual stipulates that maintenance of accounts at the
State/District/other societies is to be computerised to ensure that account statements are
prepared accurately and promptly with least efforts. However, despite about a decade of
handling Accounts by the State (RCH-I and thereafter), the Government did not
operationalise the computerised accounting system.

The Government stated that Tally 9 had been adopted from 2011-12.

The accounts of SHS did not capture transactions of receipts, expenditure, assets and
liabilities accurately and, therefore, could not be relied upon. Non-maintenance of basic
accounting records coupled with lack of proper controls on transactions adversely impacted
the quality of accounts and programme management. The financial and accounting functions
were marked by weak controls on transfer and utilisation of funds. Due to non production of
requisitioned records, audit could not vouchsafe the transactions to arrive at cases of
conclusive defalcation, misutilisation and embezzlements. Non-adherence to the prescribed
system by SHS and field units did not preclude siphoning away of funds and, therefore,
needed to be investigated.

3.15 Recommendations:

¢ — SPMU should be suitably strengthened in terms of skilled manpower and infrastructure
to improve its working; and the applicable accounting rules and manuals should be
made clearly known to the units to secure uniformity in accounting;

. Reconciliation of inter-unit/fund receipts and transfers (including direct or treasury
route releases by the Central and State Governments) should be carried out
periodically;

¢ — Transactions should not be allowed to be made outside the authorised banking
arrangement and instances coming to notice should be dealt with appropriately and
firmly;

. Number of Bank Accounts should be reduced in consultation with MoHFW, Gol to
ensure better control and monitoring; and

. FE MRs based accounting of expenditure should be replaced with SoE based accounting,
duly certified by the firm of Chartered Accountants.

available.

4.1 Infrastructure Development: Construction of buildings

NRHM aimed to bridge gaps in the existing capacity of the rural health infrastructure by
establishing functional health facilities through revitalisation of existing physical
infrastructure such as health centre buildings and fresh construction or renovation wherever
required. The Mission also sought to upgrade available facilities to Indian Public Health
Standards (IPHS), wherein the infrastructural, personnel, equipment and management
standards for different levels of health centres were defined. In Uttar Pradesh, NRHM
framework allowed expenditure up to 33 per cent of the total NRHM outlay on constructions
to remove deficiencies in public health infrastructure.

pockets of population.

4.3. Attaining Indian Public Health Standards

Indian Public Health Standards (IPHS) are envisaged to improve the quality of health care
delivery in the country. The quality of health care is to be assured through prescribed physical
and human infrastructure and its regular maintenance. The Government of India circulated
these standards for CHCs, PHCs and sub centres during 2007-11. IPHS inter alia include:

. Norms for improvement in specialist services in CHCs by ensuring availability of
specialists against all sanctioned posts and additional sanction of the posts of
Anaesthetist and Public Health Manager;

e Norms for strengthening support staff, by recruitment of Public Health Nurses and
ANMs in all Centres, in addition to existing staff;

. Norms for infrastructure, equipment, laboratory, blood storage facilities and drugs; and

. Guidelines for management of routine and emergency cases under National health
programmes, being provided at all CHCs, to maintain uniformity and also optimum
standardised treatment.

Test check of records at SPMU and DGNPME revealed that no periodical status reports
showing achievement of IPHS norms, were prepared by health facilities (CHCs, PHCs and
sub centres) in the State. Thus, ascertaining and monitoring of quality in healthcare was not
feasible during 2005-11.

Moreover, there was no ban on construction activity as Gol had only directed that the
payments be released after third party inspections of the works which was normal.

4.4 Quality of infrastructure
4.4.1 Sub centre level

To improve infrastructure at SC levels, SHS aimed to shift health facilities from rented
buildings to the Government buildings. Test check of progress reports (June 2011) of
DGNPME revealed that only 15072 of 20521 sub centres were located in the Government
buildings. Further, SHS shifted only 6170 out of 11619 sub centres from rented to
Government buildings during 2008-11'. Shifting of sub centres from rented buildings to
Government buildings did not help increase the healthcare coverage of population as no
additional sub centre was sanctioned/created during 2005-11.

While accepting the facts, the Government stated that shifting of existing sub centres from
rented building to the Government owned building was aimed at providing better medical
health facilities to common people. The Government’s reply did not address the point raised
by Audit, in regard to the stagnant number of sub centres.

delays in construction of 810 sub centres, 798 PHCs and 141 CHCs involving % 300.58 crore,
due to non-sanction of revised estimates by the Government and delay in finalisation of
MoUs with executing agencies. The Government had stated (November 2007) therein that the
remaining buildings would be completed by March 2008. Test check of records however,
revealed that delays in completion of sanctioned works persisted during 2005-11, as detailed

4.5.2 Delays in construction works executed by VHSCs

The Government and SHS sanctioned = 279.89 crore for construction of 4005 sub centres*
during 2008-09, out of State and NRHM budgets, for construction by VHSCs. Audit

had not even started in 418 (10 per cent) cases as of June 2011. Thus, 31 per cent of sub
centres remained incomplete and way behind the completion schedule of four months, despite
availability of funds. Further, DGNPME did not furnish records of follow-up action to ensure
timely construction.

The Government stated that as of October 2011, only 544 sub centres were under
construction while construction in 305 sub centres was yet to start and that DHSs and VHSCs
were responsible for delay. The reply was not acceptable as the Government should have
ensured this through monitoring and oversight.

put to use even after 12 months of completion, while 24 were not in use for 6 to 12 months
and 13 sub centres were not in use for 3-6 months. Similarly, four JSY Wards were not put to
use even after 3-12 months of completion. The details are annexed (Appendices-4.5 and 4.6).

4.7 Wasteful expenditure due to improper planning and execution

Audit observed that of 125 test checked sub centres, sanction was accorded to two new sub
centres in villages that already had sub centres. On physical verification in Etah and test
check of records in Varanasi (October 2011), Audit observed that two sub centres had been
constructed in the same Gram Sabha/village. SHS sanctioned = 8.19 lakh for construction of
a second sub centre in Ubhai Gram Sabha in Aliganj Block, Etah, during 2009-10, when a
sub centre already existed in the same Gram Sabha. The earlier sub centre was sanctioned in
2008-09. The two sub centres were located within one kilometre of each other. In another
case, one new sub centre was sanctioned® during 2009-10 in Adampur Village, Harhua Block,
Varanasi District, in place of an existing sub centre built in 1994-95, which was unutilised
(November 2011) due to encroachment. The new sub centre was sanctioned without taking
administrative action to evict the encroachers.

As pointed out in paragraph number 4.13 infra, the Medical Department did not make any
significant efforts to obtain titles for lands donated by Gram Sabhas.

° The sub centre was almost complete, as out of € 8.19 lakh, & 7.84 lakh had already been spent.

Capacity Building - Infrastructure Development , Building - Infrastructure Development

= Government accepted the fact that both the sub centres in Etah district were located in
same Gram Sabha but, however, failed to reply about the propriety of construction of two sub
centres within a kilometer of each other, in a span of two years when a large number of sub
centres continued to function from rented buildings and large number of Gram Panchayats
were without even one sub centre.

4.8 Management of works at the level of DGNPME

DGNPME transferred funds to nominated construction agencies at estimated costs, after
approval of EC. The transfer of funds was accompanied by a Memorandum of Understanding
(MoV) which stipulated the terms and conditions of engagement. Audit observed that lack of
independent technical vetting of estimates and non-implementation of MoU conditions by
DGNPME resulted in excess costing, delays, poor quality efc. as pointed out in subsequent
paragraphs.

4.8.1 Absence of formal contracts with construction agencies

Financial rules stipulate that contracting officers enter into a formal agreement with
contractors, especially for large transactions. Such contract agreements are legally vetted to
protect the Government interests. Audit observed that DGNPME entered into MoUs worth
% 1170.78 crore with construction agencies (PACCFED, LACCFED, UPPCL ec.) during
2009-11, but did not execute any formal contract agreements. Further, these MoUs were not
vetted by the Finance and Law Departments/Law Officer and were not legally enforceable.
This arrangement was fraught with risks of unfavorable terms to the department and variation
in terms and conditions of MoUs with various construction agencies over a period of time.

The Government stated that though there was no practice of signing a contract agreement or
MoU with construction agencies, DGNPME had signed MoUs with construction agencies in
the interest of work. The Government’s reply did not address the issues raised by Audit i.e.
risks, quality control, delays and penalties. Further, SPMU and not DGNPME should have
signed MoUs, as DGNPME did not feature in NRHM framework.

4.8.2 Inflated estimates

The Government orders stipulate vetting of all projects/works exceeding threshold limit by
independent technical agency like Plan Formulation and Appraisal Division (PFAD),
Department of Planning, to ensure accuracy and economy in costing and conformity with
PWD specifications/standards.

Audit observed that estimates of major works like strengthening of District Hospitals’ were
not vetted by PFAD or any other independent technical agency. Further, UPPCL had not
responded for vetting of detailed estimate by PFAD despite issue of reminder® in February
2011. Similarly, model estimates for construction of sub centres and JSY Wards during
2009-11 were not vetted by PFAD/independent appraising body, even though the cost of all
proposed constructions was हैं 456.49 crore’. This ran the risk of excess costing and faulty
technical design.

” State Government rules require vetting of all proposals costing more than & 40.00 lakh by PFAD and projects costing more than & 2 crore
by Expenditure Finance Committee.

provision for external site development was not admissible.

d) 35 regards RFPTCs, the Government stated that the Schedule of Rates (SoR) of PWD
for framed structures had been adopted. The reply was incorrect as PWD’s SoR for
framed structures were applicable only for buildings of six or more floors, whereas
RFPTCs were to be constructed up to three floors.

4.8.3 Non-adherence to UP PWD’s local Schedule of Rates (SoRs)

DGNPME prepared model estimates for civil works based on PWD’s SoR applicable to
Lucknow circle, while drawing up the State PIP and for transfer of funds to nominated

' JSY Ward is a ‘small size’ building with area 50 m? which is less than that of sub centres (74.89 का).

Performance Audit Report on National Rural Health Mission for the year ended 31 March 2011 | 65


Capacity Building - Infrastructure Development , Building - Infrastructure Development

= agencies. However, the construction agencies were required to prepare detailed
estimates on PWD’s local SoRs, applicable to concerned districts/circles in which
construction was to be undertaken. Further, the construction agencies were to render audited
expenditure against these detailed estimates.

Audit observed that construction agencies did not prepare detailed estimates on the basis of
local PWD SoRs, which resulted in undue advantage to construction agencies, as local SoRs
were generally lower than Lucknow’s SoR. For instance, application of local PWD SoR to
model estimates of construction of health facilities (Sub centres, JSY Wards, Training
Centres and District Level Hospitals) in Allahabad, Bareilly, Budaun and Mirzapur districts
would have saved % 4.90 crore (Appendix-4.12).

The Government while accepting the audit observation stated that recovery in all such cases
would be made after collecting information in this regard.

4.8.4 Avoidable expenditure

DGNPME awarded the work of supply of equipments/material to nominated construction
agencies for = 108.04 crore during 2009-11. The entrustment of these works entailed extra
cost due to centage charges. Since such supplies were outside the scope of civil works,
DGNPME could have directly procured the same from the market and saved हैं 13.50 crore
(Appendix—4.13).

DGNPME stated that it was not paying centage charges on supply of material and
equipment. The reply was incorrect as the MoUs with construction agencies provided for
centage and the headquarters of construction agencies had released funds to unit offices after
deducting centage charges.

4.8.5 Excess release of advance

SHS released funds to DGNPME for onward transfer to construction agencies. DGNPME
was to release these funds to construction agencies in a phased manner on fulfillment of
certain conditionalities in the MoU. Audit, however, observed that DGNPME did not ensure
adherence to MoU conditions as detailed below:

a) DGNPME released 100 per cent of the sanctioned amount of हैं 203.78 crore to
PACCFED during 2009-10 without obtaining detailed estimates and utilisation
certificates;

b) DGNPME released 100 per cent of the sanctioned amount of % 38.57" crore to
Construction and Design Services (C&DS), U. P. Jal Nigam, during
August 2009 — March 2010, without obtaining detailed estimates and utilisation
certificates.

The Government agreed that initially phased transfer of funds was done as stipulated in
the MoU, but later funds were transferred to the construction agencies in violation of
the MoU and the released amounts were treated as expenditure.

On 22 August 2009: ₹ 893.51 lakh, 03 September 2009: ₹ 109.83 lakh, 21 January 2010: ₹ 984,93 lakh and 31 March 2010:
₹ 1868.40 lakh ~ Total: ₹ 38.57 crore.

66 | Performance Audit Report on National Rural Health Mission for the year ended 31 March 2011


Capacity Building - Infrastructure Development , Building - Infrastructure Development

= Test check of release orders revealed that while DGNPME had deducted Income Tax

from funds released to construction agencies, it had not deducted VAT at the rate of
two per cent (Appendix—4.14) which led to a loss of revenue to the State Government
on one hand and unauthorised aid of ~ 18.95 crore to construction agencies on the
other.

The Government stated that these were advances and not payments to contractors. Hence,
Tax Deducted at Source (TDS) on account of VAT was not to be deducted. Further, in many
cases the payments to these agencies were 100 per cent of the estimated costs. Audit did not
come across any case of adjustments of VAT in subsequent releases made by DGNPME to
these agencies for old or new works. Moreover, as per the letter no. 7795-3 dated 16
December 2009 of SPMU any release made to executing agencies was to be treated as
expenditure and not as advance.

4.8.6 Loss of interest on idle funds with construction agencies

Though the MoU provided for credit of interest earned on client/department’s funds to
client’s account, DGNPME did not enforce this by calling for project accounts from
construction agencies. Further, DGNPME transferred funds before signing of MoU,
adversely affecting dates on which accrual of interest on idle funds would have started. Some
instances are given below:

. & 291.38 crore remained with construction agencies as idle funds, for periods ranging
from 4 to 22 months. This led to loss of interest of = 8.15 crore (Appendix—4.15).

. Medical Department failed to hand over land to PACCFED in 540 cases of construction
of sub centres during 2009-11. Since, DGNPME did not get the funds refunded for such
works, PACCFED received an undue advantage of % 1.14 crore on account of interest
earned on idle funds (Appendix—4.16).

. DGNPME released हैं 337.57 crore, between March 2010 and March 2011, to
PACCFED (Appendix—4.17). This enabled PACCFED to earn interest on transferred
funds of = 5.05 crore (% 2.42 + % 2.63 crore) as MoU mandated accountal of interest
earned, only from the date of signing of MoU (Appendix—4.18 & 4.19).

In reply, the Government stated that instructions had been issued to deposit the accrued
interest on such released amounts and one of the construction agency, viz. UPPCL had
already refunded (November 2011) हैं 6.16 crore of accrued interest.

4.9 Release of funds in March

As per NRHM framework, non-committed unspent balances as on 31 March in any year are
adjusted in next year’s releases. SHS and DGNPME released % 93.31 crore to construction
agencies on 27 March 2010, even without signing the MoUs for completion of work in that
financial year, which in any case was not possible given the date of release. SHS and
DGNPME repeated this in 2010-11 and released % 155.26 crore on 01 March 2011 without
signing MoUs. This resulted in inflated expenditure for those years, to avoid adjustment
against non-committed unspent balances.

Performance Audit Report on National Rural Health Mission for the year ended 31 March 2011 | 67


Capacity Building - Infrastructure Development , Building - Infrastructure Development

Es Government in its reply did not address the core issue highlighted in the audit
observation. Instead it stated that funds were released in March as per work plan and signing
of MoUs was not a practice in the State and there was no Government order which stipulated
that funds will be released only on signing of MoU. The reply was factually incorrect as State
Government’s Departments had been signing MoUs with construction agencies in
pre-NRHM period also and this violation of MoU signed by the Directorate cannot be
justified on propriety or compliance grounds.

4.10 Loss due to non-imposition of penalty

The MoU between DGNPME and construction agencies provided for imposition of penalty
for delay in completion of sub centres and drug warehouses, at the rate of 0.1 per cent per
day, for delay in completion of JSY Wards, ANMTCs and RFPTCs at the rate of 0.1 per cent
per day subject to a maximum of 5 per cent of estimated costs and 0.1 per cent per week for
delay in upgradation of CHCs. DGNPME, however, did not invoke this provision.

This resulted in an unauthorised aid of हैं 22.31 crore as of 30 June 2011 as detailed in
Appendix—4.20. Further, test check of records in 17 districts revealed that & 2,097 crore was
leviable as penalty as of 30 September 2011 from construction agencies for delay in
construction of health facilities (Appendix-4.21).

In reply, the Government stated that details of work-wise delays were being compiled and
necessary action would be taken.

4.11 Non-compliance with provisions of MoUs

Audit observed that DGNPME did not adhere to MoU conditions which resulted in delays,
poor quality, excess costing efc. Some instances of non-compliance are as below:

a) A joint Programme Evaluation and Review Technique (PERT) chart was to be prepared
to ensure timely completion of work and to show critical obligations of each party
during different stages of construction. No such chart was, however, prepared;

The Government stated that the preparation of work-wise joint PERT chart or Bar chart
was not possible. The reply was incorrect, as preparation of PERT charts was a
necessity as per condition of the MoUs signed by DGNPME with these agencies to
ensure timely completion of work in an accountable manner;

b) Construction agencies were to submit detailed project reports within a month of receipt
of letters of intent. Such reports were generally not submitted, which implied that works
were undertaken without detailed estimates, technical sanction efc.; and

The Government stated that DGNPME was not aware of such cases. The reply was
incorrect as DGNPME was aware of non-preparation of detailed estimates by
construction agencies and even advised (April 2010) PACCFED to this effect. Further,
the reply highlighted the weak controls and monitoring mechanism of DGNPME. Thus,
there was no system in place in DGNPME to watch the progress of execution of work
without technical sanction;

"Sub centres € 124.55 lakh, JSY Wards ¥ 4.02 lakh, Drug Warchouse रैं 65.74 lakh, CHC ₹ 7.94 and ANMTC ₹ 6.20 lakh.

In respect of periodical audited accounts, the Government replied that necessary
instructions had already been issued to construction agencies to produce the records.

4.12 Quality assurance by Medical and Health Department

DGNPME was to ensure quality of works awarded to construction agencies. The Government
Order’ relating to empanelment of different Government authorised construction agencies
stipulated that PWD would undertake quarterly inspection/review of work done by these
agencies. GO ibid also mandated Technical Audit Cell/Task Force at different levels to
inspect works. However, DGNPME did not incorporate these references of GOs in MoUs.
Notwithstanding the above lapse, DGNPME could still have enforced this provision by virtue
of cited GO. However, DGNPME did not ensure periodical inspection of works by PWD
engineers or the Technical Audit Cell.

The Medical, Health and Family Welfare Department has an Engineering Cell in the office of
DGMH, Assistant Engineer (AE) in the offices of Assistant Directors (Health) and Junior
Engineers in the offices of CMOs. Audit observed that JEs were inspecting sites infrequently.
There were no clear and prescribed norms for inspections or manuals to guide JEs.

Subsequent to transfer of Gram Sabha land to Medical Department for construction of sub

disputes. However, on physical verification of 125 sub centres test-checked (Appendix-4.3)

Department. Audit also observed that the required approval of the municipal corporations/
development authorities for layout plans for constructions in urban areas had not been
obtained rendering these constructions unauthorised.

The Government stated that necessary instructions had been issued and in future work would
be started only on transfer of title of land.

GoUP laid down (January 2007) a system for award of the Government contracts in an
open, transparent and fair manner, with restrictive provisions for contractors with
criminal/mafia background.

4.14.2 Award of civil works solely on nomination basis

Audit observed that during 2009-11, the EC of SHS disregarded the above provisions and
awarded 6004 works amounting to हैं 1170.78 crore to four State Government corporations
and cooperative societies on nomination basis.

The action of EC was flawed as:

a) There was no approval of funding agency (Gol), as required under MoU between Gol
and GoUP, bye-laws of SHS and draft Finance and Accounts Manual;

b) Award of work on nomination basis was against CVC circular, State Government’s
order'® and judgement of Hon’ble Supreme Court in Meerut Nagar Nigam case’”;

c) This was in violation of even SHS’s own delegation of financial powers order dated
1 March 2008, whereby UPRNN was designated the primary construction agency
and any other agency could be considered only when that agency would work on
competitive basis;

d) Work was awarded without committing construction agencies to execute works on
open tender basis; and

e) Two Cooperative Societies and one Government company were preferentially
awarded works despite their inherent weaknesses as brought out in subsequent
paragraphs. Similarly, work relating to ‘Strengthening of District Hospitals’ was
arbitrarily awarded during 2010-11, as brought in the para 4.15 below.

4.14.3 Preference for Cooperative Societies

EC awarded works amounting to = 835.09 crore to PACCFED and LACCFED during
2009-11.EC’s actions were flawed as:

a) Cooperative Societies had weak public accountability systems as compared to State
PWD and the Government corporations as they were not subject to same level of
legislative scrutiny and administrative control. They did not have strong accounting
and auditing systems. They were also not under the ambit of RTI Act, 2005, or
subject to C&AG’s audit.

b) These organisations had weak organisational, financial and technical capabilities
(Appendix- 4.22) and did not get any work except from the State Government in the
last five years. They were awarded works in spite of not very encouraging
credentials. For example, the Department of Rural Development had barred’

did not involve conflict of interest, rather helped in synergising activities.

4.14.4 Antecedents of UPPCL

EC awarded works amounting to % 312.17 crore to UPPCL during 2008-10. This was done
without considering the past track record of the agency. A special audit’? of UPPCL in
2007, by the Office of Principal Accountant General (Civil Audit) Uttar Pradesh, found
serious deficiencies in its working. The details are given below:

a) UPPCL was declared (14 July 1999) the executing agency without concurrence of the
Finance Department and without ascertaining its technical efficiency;

b) Law Department’s concurrence had not been obtained for the format of MoUs and
the MoUs did not contain any penal clause;

c) Despite being operational for more than 30 years, UPPCL was yet to frame its rules
and procedures. It neither maintained a list of approved contractors nor followed a
system of selection on the basis of tendered rates. Instead labour contractors called
Piece Rate Workers (PRWs) were selected on the basis of personal contact by local
staff;

d) Work orders far in excess of delegated powers were issued and more than one work
order was issued in favour of the same PRW, in contravention of financial rules;

e) Quality of works was also not checked adequately by PWD’s Technical Audit Cell
(TAC); and

f) _ Fifty eight per cent of 155 works due for completion up to 31 March, 2007 remained
incomplete at the time of audit.

) Finance Department requested for special audit of UPPCL vide its letter no. Vitt Audit Prakosth (Si/Re)/253/x-10(141)/2006-07 dated
15 February 2007. The report of special audit was submitted to the Government vide letter no. PAG (CA)/Sr. DAG (W-ID/Sp
Audit/MoU/61 dated 17 January 2008.

72 | Performance Audit Report on National Rural Health Mission for the year ended 31 March 2011


Capacity Building - Infrastructure Development , Building - Infrastructure Development

= Arbitrary selection of construction agencies

The PIP for 2010-11 had a provision of हैं 89 crore for upgradation of 89 District Level
Hospitals. The scope of work included supply and installation of modular operation
theatres. EC decided (July 2010) to get this work done through PACCFED. This was done
after eliminating UPPCL for unsatisfactory performance of the same work in 40 other
hospitals since March 2010 but without considering the relative strengths and past
experience in supply of MOTs by different empanelled agencies. EC subsequently reversed
its earlier decision and decided (August 2010) to award the works to UPPCL on the ground
that it had done survey work and was satisfactorily executing the same work awarded
earlier. Within a month, EC again restored (September 2010) the original decision on the
ground that UPPCL’s performance was poor and change was recommended by DGNPME.
The oscillatory change of construction agencies within a short span of time, with
contradictory facts was a manifestation of ad hoc and arbitrary decision making at EC
level.

Regarding the system of procurement of works, the Government stated that though the
Government Order dated 01 March 2008 envisaged execution of work through UP Rajkiya
Nirman Nigam (UPRNN), this actually meant any Government construction agency, as
UPRNN was not capable of executing all Government works. All the Government companies
and corporations which were awarded works by EC were also declared the Government
agencies by the Finance Department. The work was awarded to construction agencies as a
deposit work and there was no provision for competition between the various Government
companies/corporations. The reply was not acceptable as at the time of issuing the order
nominating UPRNN, the Government was aware of the existence of other companies and
corporations and yet did not empanel them. The objective of nominating a competent and
tested agency after due consideration, was defeated by the convenient interpretation of orders.

4.15 Deficiencies at the level of construction agencies
4.15.1 Quality Control
Audit observed the following deficiencies relating to quality in execution of work:

. Works Departments in the Government ensures quality through a regime involving
works manual, quality control labs in each division or through dedicated quality control
divisions, submission of quality control reports with each bill, system of ‘check
measurement’, independent quality audit and finally independent quality check by
Technical Wing in Central Vigilance Commission or PWD’s Technical Audit Cell.
Since no such system existed in these construction agencies, the works assigned to these
agencies were fraught with risk of substandard work;

. Quality of work was also to be ensured through engagement of technically competent
contractors as contractors were to be screened through the system of ‘technical bid’
which assesses organisational, financial and technical competence of the bidders.
However, construction agencies like UPPCL, PACCFED and LACCFED did not follow
the system of open tendering or two bid system. These agencies executed works by
procuring materials and concluding labour contracts on piece work rate;

Performance Audit Report on National Rural Health Mission for the year ended 31 March 2011 | 73


Capacity Building - Infrastructure Development , Building - Infrastructure Development

= PACCFED and LACCFED purchased material without conforming to principles of

competition and transparency as outlined in CVC circulars and the Government
policies. Further, brand names/specifications/quality of materials like steel, cement and
bricks procured for construction works were not mentioned in purchase/supply orders.
In some test checked cases (Appendix-4.23) even copies of purchase/supply orders
were not available. Therefore, neither the quality of material used in these constructions
nor the quantity actually purchased could be ensured;

. These construction agencies did not have camp offices or stores in every district where
they were executing works. Their offices were mostly located in divisional towns.
Further, they were not maintaining Material at Site (MAS) accounts to record issue and
consumption of stores. The non-availability of store, site office and non-maintenance of
MAS account, point towards doubtful receipt and consumption;

. One member of the client department, as per the order of PACCFED, was to be
nominated to purchase committee of construction agency. However, this was not done,
resulting in lack of assurance about the price, quality and quantity of purchased goods;

. As per PACCFED’s order dated 10 September 1998, the Executive Engineer was
required to be present at the time of laying slabs, to provide assurance on use of
requisite quantity of steel. Audit observed that Measurement Books did not show the
presence of the Executive Engineer at the time of laying slabs, in any of the
test-checked districts;

. Building works required around 11 tests, as per standard bid documents of building
construction agencies, to be carried out at various frequencies (Appendix-4.24) to
ensure quality work. On the question of the status of mandatory quality control tests,
most of the divisions did not produce evidence of any third party material test reports. A
few divisions produced test reports related to crushing test etc., however, there was no
evidence of these tests being conducted at the normative frequency;

. Orders issued by PACCFED required conduct of quality control tests through renowned
institutions and submission of test reports to PACCFED headquarters. Audit observed
that quality controls tests were not got done through renowned institutions and copies of
test reports were not sent to headquarters;

e Construction agencies were to provide plants, machinery and tools required for
execution of works to Piece Rate Work (PRW) contractors. Audit observed that in
17 test-checked districts (for physical verification of civil works); there was no
documentation of issue and receipt of plants, machinery and tools to and from PRWs.
As a result possibility of sub-standard work being executed without requisite machinery
could not be ruled out.

The Government did not examine the audit observations and left it to construction agencies to
clarify the issues. Further, only two construction agencies (PACCFED, UPPCL) responded to
audit observations. They admitted that they did not follow open tender system and executed
works on Piece Rate Work (PRW) system. They also claimed availability of quality control
labs at zonal (PACCFED) or at headquarter levels. The contention of construction agencies

74 | Performance Audit Report on National Rural Health Mission for the year ended 31 March 2011


Capacity Building - Infrastructure Development , Building - Infrastructure Development

i not acceptable as no documentary evidence of periodical quality control tests in all works
was furnished in support of their argument.

e — Test check of MBs revealed that the measurements were mostly recorded in one day
after completion of work, which renders the accuracy of measurements questionable, as
material consumption for works like foundation laying, erection of the walls etc. cannot
be measured post construction. PACCFED accepted the fact that measurements in sub
centres were done on a single day due to volume of work and remoteness of
construction sites.

Thus, the absence of a strong quality control system resulted in wide variations of
consumption of material in fully constructed buildings. While under-consumption was
indicative of sub-standard work, over-consumption was indicative of suspect purchases or
pilferage. The details are given below:

a) Consumption of cement, steel and stone grit was excess in the range of 2.49 to
112 per cent in five districts (Appendix-4.25);

b) Inferior bricks were used in construction work (Sub centres, JSY Wards, Drug
Warehouses and Training centres) during 2009-11 in Agra. This resulted in construction
of sub-standard health facilities costing हैं 9.48 crore (Appendix 4-26).

In reply, the Government stated that M-100 bricks had been used on account of the locally
available soil.

The reply was not acceptable as bricks were a major component of the building and affected
its strength and only M-150 bricks were permissible, which had also been provisioned for in
the estimates.

4.15.2 Procurement system in Construction agencies

SHS bye-laws, CVC guidelines and GOs stipulate open, transparent and fair procurement
system by all Government, semi-government and autonomous bodies. MoUs with
construction agencies provided for association of DGNPME in tendering processes for
NRUM related procurements. Test check of procurements by construction agencies revealed
that procurement was not competitive and that the technical qualifications of selected firms
were questionable. The details are given below:

Purchase of Modular OTs hy PACCFED

DGNPME awarded (January 2011) work of upgradation of District Hospitals in 89 districts
for = 315.25 crore to PACCFED. This work inter alia included supply and installation of
pre-fabricated Modular Operation Theatres (MOTs), at हैं 75 lakh per site. PACCFED
awarded the work of supply and installation of MOTs to M/s C.N.C. Metal Forming Limited,
Gajraula, J.P. Nagar, at = 75 lakh per unit. Test check of process of procurement revealed
irregularities in both award and execution of work as detailed below:

a) PACCFED did not resort to open tendering despite (i) contract value being
र₹ 58.50 crore, (ii) the specialised nature of work and (iii) the Government orders/SHS

4.15.4 Non-compliance with labour regulations

Nominated construction agencies were awarded works at estimated costs. The estimated costs
were based on PWD’s SoR, which provided for expenses by the contractor, for fulfillment of
statutory labour obligations. Audit observed that nominated construction agencies did not
ensure compliance with labour regulations by their contractors. This benefitted construction
agencies as they could hire labour at lower costs as compared to estimates. The details are
given below:

. Construction agencies were required to get themselves registered with Labour
Department in compliance with Building and Other Construction Workers’ Welfare
Cess Act, 1996 and rules thereunder (February 2009). However, Audit observed that
unit 3 of PACCFED, Lucknow, got the registration in September 2011. Further, test
check revealed that these divisions had not deducted labour cess from the payments to
labour contractors/PRWs, which was to be deposited into cess account; and

e As per terms and conditions of the work order given to PRWs, they were to comply
with labour regulations and also to PWD/CPWD specifications. Test check revealed
that PRW contractors did not maintain any records to show compliance with labour
laws and had no documents regarding registration of PRWs with the Labour
Department and unlike CPWD divisions, no Labour Inspector was appointed, despite
the fact that they were only engaged in labour contracts. There was no report on PRWs’
regular and due payments to labourers, supported with vouchers.

PACCFED accepted that cess was not being deducted and that it would be done in future.
Further, its unit offices had been instructed to comply with labour regulations. Audit
observed that the Medical Department, however, disassociated itself from any responsibility,
stating that the issue concerned to the executing agencies.

4.16 Construction through VHSCs in 2008-09

An allotment of = 80 crore was included in PIP of 2008-09, for construction of sub centres.
SPMU released (May 2008) % 80 crore from NRHM funds directly to DHS for construction
of 1142 sub centres in 35 districts. In addition, State funds (Grant Numbers 35 and 83) were
also released for construction of 2863 sub centres. CMOs of the concerned districts were to
transfer these funds to VHSCs, who were to construct building as per guidelines. As funds
were not transferred to VHSCs in seven districts, the Government directed (March 2009)

78 | Performance Audit Report on National Rural Health Mission for the year ended 31 March 2011


Capacity Building - Infrastructure Development , Building - Infrastructure Development

= to transfer a sum of % 6.21 crore, out of the non-transferred NRHM funds, to
UPPCL for construction of 90 sub centres, out of 260 approved for these districts.

As per guidelines issued by the Government’ construction works were to be carried out
through daily wage labourers, the Gram Pradhan/ANM were to purchase material by inviting
quotations and cash book, material register, muster roll, inspection register, day-to-day
progress register etc. were to be maintained at VHSC.

Test check of records in the offices of CMOs and Block PHCs revealed delays in
construction of sub centres as pointed out in paragraph 4.5 of this report and other
deficiencies related to poor record keeping, poor quality and monitoring as discussed in
succeeding paragraphs.

4.16.1 Deficiency in guidelines

Scrutiny of guidelines revealed that it did not provide for (a) sanctioning the required number
of labourers at different stages of work (labour mandays); (b) maintenance of muster roll
issue register; and (c) maintaining of measurement book (MBs) to measure executed work.
This resulted in non-accountal of work done and wide variations in labour mandays.

4.16.2 Non-maintenance of records

Test check of records of 31 sub centres”* in four test checked districts which produced
records to Audit, revealed that it did not maintain cash book, cheque issue register, material
register, inspection register efc. as envisaged in the guidelines. The records of 11 VHSCs”
were not produced to Audit.

Audit observed that in most sub centres, payment vouchers in respect of commercial ply flush
door, aluminum fitting with screws for doors and windows shutters, MS section glazed
windows, water proof cement for second coat painting, material for one coat primer/two coats
distemper efc. were not found, which rendered the claims about quantity and quality of
material consumed doubtful.

4.16.3 Quality control

Guidelines envisaged achievement of quality in work through training of VHSC
functionaries, maintenance of records and system of technical inspections. However, these
provisions were not utilised. Physical inspection of three sub centres test-checked in two
districts” revealed poor quality of work, as evidenced from incomplete works, cracks and
poorly finished exteriors.

Besides the wide variations in consumption of material in the 31 test checked sub centres
listed in previous paragraph, are tabulated below. While a reasonable margin of 10 per cent
may be acceptable, the variations were beyond the envelope of acceptability. Under
consumption could be a pointer to inferior work, while excess consumptions point towards

Non-maintenance of records and lack of supervision by Junior Engineers/CMOs/Executive

4.17 Recommendations:

. The Government may take immediate action to tighten norms for selection of agencies
and for award of works. Even if work is to be awarded only to the Government
agencies, competitiveness and transparency must still be ensured and experience and
capabilities must be given due weightage;

. While releasing funds, attention must be paid to comparability with industry norms or
market rates to ensure reasonableness of rates and to guard against overcharging,
leakages and excess expenditure; and

. The Government should strengthen its quality control mechanisms to ensure that
execution of works is of the desired quality and are as per schedule.

5 Capacity Building - Human Resource

5.1 Introduction

The Mission aimed to increase the availability of manpower through provision of trained
female community health worker, ASHA, at village level, a minimum of two ANMs at each
sub centre and three staff nurses at every PHC to ensure round the clock availability of
services. The Out Patient Department (OPD) at PHC was to be strengthened through posting/
appointment of AYUSH doctors over and above the Medical Officers posted there. The State
Government was to fill up the existing vacancies by new contractual appointments for which
Gol support was available. CHCs were to be brought at par with the Indian Public Health
Standards (IPHS) norms to provide round the clock services by providing seven Specialists
and nine staff nurses in every CHC. A separate AYUSH set up was also to be provided in
each CHC/ PHC.

In reply, the Government stated that IPHS norms would be implemented in future.

The requirement of health workers for rural health facilities as per IPHS norms vis-d-vis the
personnel available there against were as below:

82 | Performance Audit Report on National Rural Health Mission for the year ended 31 March 2011


Capacity Building - Human Resource , Building - Human Resource

= Accredited Social Health Activist (ASHA)
5.3.1 Availability of ASHA

NRHM framework envisages providing one ASHA in every village with a population of
1000. ASHA was the interface between the community and the public health system and the
nodal person for assessing the local health needs. The data collected by them for preparation
of grass root level health plans, constituted inputs for the State Health Action Plan (SHAP).
ASHAs were to undergo five modules of induction training, apart from periodic skill
up-gradation and provided drug kits to enhance their utility and credibility. ASHAs paid from
the Mission flexipool were to reinforce community action for universal immunisation, safe
delivery, new born care, prevention of water-borne and other communicable diseases,
nutrition and sanitation. ASHA was also to help promote preventive health by converging
activities of nutrition, education, drinking water, sanitation etc.

Audit observed that against the requirement of 1,36,268 ASHAs only 1,36,183 ASHAs were
selected as of 31 March 2011. Out of the selected ASHAs, only 1,11,058 (82 per cent) had
completed all the five modules of training. Further, the data for 2005-11, collected from
21700 of 22 test checked districts, revealed shortages up to 24 per cent ASHAs during
2005-06 (Appendix-5.1). In six districts” adequate numbers of ASHAs were not selected for
training.

The Government stated that till date 1,21,580 ASHAs had been trained.

5.3.2 Purchase of ASHA kits

As provided in State PIPs, every year ASHAs were to be provided with a kit, including items
like Oral Rehydration Solution (ORS), sets of 10 basic medicines and contraceptives. The
State PIPs included following provisions for procurement of ASHA Kits:

Table 5.4 : Provisions for procurement of ASHA Kits

‘um! its to be procured and rate per unit

2009-10 6.75 1.35 lakh kits @ ₹500 each
2010-11° 12.94 2.58 lakh kits @ ₹500 each

(Source : State PIPs of 2007-08, 2008-09, 2009-10 and 2010-11)

Audit observed that no kits were procured during 2005-06, 2006-07, 2007-08 and 2010-11.
Only 1,32,995 kits and 1,31,684 kits were procured during 2008-09 and 2009-10
respectively. Thus, during four years, ASHAs worked without the prescribed kit.

The Government accepted release of fund to the districts for purchase of ASHA kits only
during 2008-09 and 2009-10 for purchase of 1,32.995 and 1,31, 684 kits respectively.

Capacity Building - Human Resource , Building - Human Resource

= kits were to comprise 12 items including Iron Folic Acid (IFA) Tablets (large). SPMU
decided (07 July 2008) to purchase IFA tablets (large) centrally and retained हैं 125 per kit.
Balance % 375 per kit was released to the districts for procurement of remaining items locally.
CMOs informed SPMU that ~ 375 was insufficient to procure remaining items.
Consequently, the Mission Director, SPMU, revised (August 2008) the quantity of some
items to be included in the kit as tabulated below reducing their effectiveness:

Table 5.5 : Quantity of items in ASHA Kits

Disposable Delivery Kit 20 10
ORS Packet (WHO) 100 10
Cotton 500 gms 200 gms

(Source : Information furnished by SPMU)

As a result requisite medicines prescribed in the kits were not provided to ASHAs for
providing effective services.

Further, हैं 12.94 crore was released (November 2010) to DGNPME for purchase of IFA
tablets at the rate of हैं 125 per kit. DGNPME returned the amount to SPMU (April 2011)
stating that the tender was cancelled as the rates received were higher than those prescribed in
PIP. Thus, no IFA tablets were purchased during 2008-11 for ASHA kits compromising the
effectiveness of ASHAs.

The Government stated that certain items were reduced from the kit as the institutional
deliveries had increased. The reply was not correct as contraceptive pills, used for spacing the
pregnancies, were to be distributed among rural women; and cotton and ORS packets are
used also for emergencies other than deliveries. Thus, the increase in institutional deliveries
did not reduce the requirement of the kit. Moreover, PIPs did not mention the reduction
effected.

5.4 Sub centres

Two female ANMs and one Multi Purpose Worker-Male (MPW-M) were to be posted in
each sub-centre. The position of availability of the ANM and MPW-M was not furnished. As
per the RHS, 2010, the deployment of ANM and MPW-M for 20521 sub centres was short by
58 and 90 per cent respectively against the IPHS norms as of March 2010.

The Government, while accepting the observation stated that 3276 sub centres were running
without ANMs and 2489 (28 per cent) Multi Purpose Workers were available against 8853
sanctioned posts.

The vacancies of regular posts were to be filled by the State and the additional posts required
for NRHM activities by contractual staff to be paid out of NRHM funds. The target in PIPs of
2009-10 and 2010-11 for appointment of ANMs on contract and achievement thereof were as
under:

= Training

NRHM Framework stipulates that the implementation teams, particularly at the district and
State level, would require development of specific skills. The State level Resource Centre
will be identified to enable innovations and impart new technical skills. Further, the
investment required was to be identified to successfully carry out the training/ sensitization
programmes. However, SPMU did not intimate whether any resource centre was identified.

Gol, MoHFW Review Report (May 2011) mentioned that the State designated the State
Institute of Health and Family Welfare (579) as Collaborating Training Institute (CTI) to
coordinate with Directorates, other training institutes and agencies for design and
implementation of training all over the State.

The Government did not offer any comment in this regard.

5.9 Posting of junior officers as Chief Medical Officers

The post of CMO, FW was created (May 2010), without taking into account that one CMO
was already available in each district and was abolished (April 2011) within one year of its
creation citing coordination problems. Further, the Hon’ble High Court of Judicature at
Allahabad directed (12 January 2011) the State Government to adhere to “rule of seniority”
while selecting level-4 officers for posting as CMOs. Notwithstanding this direction, junior
officers continued to be posted as CMOs. Thus, the judicial pronouncement was not being
followed in true spirit.

Staffing may be ensured through regular and contractual recruitments to achieve IPHS
norms; and training courses, as per training need analysis, may be ensured.

6 Procurement - State Level

6.1 Procurement System

Timely supply of drugs and equipment of good quality, involving procurement as well as
logistics management, is of critical importance in any health system. NRHM, therefore,
envisaged streamlining procurement and logistics to ensure availability of drugs and
medicines at all health centres.

The capacity for decentralised procurement at the district level was to be developed. A State
led initiative for capacity building and setting up of State Procurement Systems and
Distribution Networks for improved supplies and distribution were planned in the framework.
SHM and DHMs were to establish a transparent and accountable system in procurement,
stores and logistics. Fifty per cent of the streamlining to ensure availability of drugs and
medicines at sub centres/PHC/CHC was to be done by 2007 and the entire process was to be
completed by 2008. An external assessment of the outcome of the streamlining process was
also to be conducted. The procurement system of Gol was to be relied upon in the interim till
capacities were developed at State/district/block levels with an emphasis on timely,
transparent and quality procurements.

6.1.1 Weakness of procurement system in the State

The procurement policy of the State has been under severe criticism by various agencies. The
World Bank in its report on UP State Financial Accountability Assessment 2004 had found
loose controls in the system and absence of procurement rules as well as of a central agency
to develop policies and norms. The State planning Board (SPB) in the 11" Five Year Plan
documents (2007-12) for UP in chapter on Governance, observed that the procurement
system of drugs and medical equipment was marked by delays, lack of transparency and
flourishing corruption.

6.1.2 Risk areas of procurement under NRHM

The adverse observations of the World Bank and SPB on the procurement process under the
Government system need to be viewed in the light of high risk involved in further relaxed
procedure being followed under NRHM as discussed below.

. Full powers to EC of SHS in purchases as against limited financial powers to the
departmental heads in the State Government; and

. Involvement of only one Principal Secretary level officer in decision making body (EC)
of SHS for high value contracts against three Principal Secretary level officers in the
Government including Finance and Planning Department in EFC before approval by the
Cabinet.

Performance Audit Report on National Rural Health Mission for the year ended 31 March 2011 | 93


Procurement - State Level

is Non-adherence to NRHM guidelines/Diversions from NRHM goals

Despite high risks involved in procurement, even the provisions of NRHM guidelines were
not adhered to the following extent :

° Decentralisation of procurement to the district level by setting up State Procurement
System and Distribution networks, which was required to be completed by 2008, had.
not been taken up to secure improved supplies and distribution;

° A Procurement Cell at SPMU for strengthening the procurement process, which was to
be headed by one Procurement Specialist with supporting staff, was not set up as of
November, 2011 even though a budgetary provision of हैं 1.13 crore was made in PIP of
2008-09. This enabled technical Directorates to act as procurement agents. The details
are discussed later in the chapter.

° Contracts for procurement of goods and construction were awarded to seven
Government sponsored agencies on nomination basis without following any
competitive and open bid system;

¢ — Award of work on nomination basis was also against CVC circular’, the State
Government’s order” and Hon’ble Supreme Court’s judgment in Meerut Nagar Nigam

3

case";

e Past experience and organizational and technical capabilities of nominated agencies
were not considered to ensure timely and quality delivery; and

e The reasonability of rates was not ensured while outsourcing services.

6.1.4 Procurement framework

SHS bye-laws provide that procurement from the money received from Gol for NRHM
programmes will be carried out as per recommended policy of Gol. Any procurement; in
cases wherein Gol had authorised GoUP, including procurement of services and goods;
would be prioritised in the following order:

e DGS&D rate contract, if not available then;

° Rate contract of other organisation of Gol, if not available then;
° Rate contract approved by GoUP, if not available then;

° Tender procedure recommended by Gol.

Audit observed that the Executive Committee (EC) and High Power Committee authorised
DGNPME to make procurements which in turn authorised various agencies to make
procurements. This authorisation was done on nomination basis. The nominated agencies in
turn procured goods and services through a process which was not in consonance with open

6.1.5 Procurement through Technical Directorates

In the absence of Procurement Cell within SPMU, Technical Directorates like DGNPME,
DGMH and DGME became procurement agents. They, in turn released funds to State
Government companies (UPSIC) and cooperative societies (Cooperative Press Federation,
NCCF, LACCFED-Panchayat Udyog) on nomination basis. DGNPME functioned as the
primary procurement agency‘ for SHS/SPMU. The Executive Committee (EC) released funds

6.1.6 Procurement decisions

A codified purchase manual, containing detailed purchase guidelines, procedures and
delegation of powers is necessary to ensure systematic and uniform approach in decision-
making relating to procurements. However, in the absence of procurement manual, standard.
formats for submission of financial proposals and their independent financial vetting by the
proposed Procurement Cell within SHS, there was no transparency and consistency in the
decisions of EC.

Proposals were put up to EC by the Programme Managers in SPMU without vetting by
independent professionals, unlike in Government where PFAD plays this role. The agenda
papers of EC meetings were generally deficient and did not have any details, timelines by
