ag the procurement was to be made, specifications of the items to be procured,
competence of the agencies being considered, lessons learnt in past in similar procurements,
risks in procurement, utilisation of stores etc. The decisions of EC did not have any reference
to the procurement policy adopted and justification for the procurement decision. Further, no
formal sanctions based on EC’s decisions were issued.

The Government stated that EC under SHS had full administrative and financial powers to
sanction all financial proposals. It was stated further that procurement related funds were
placed at the disposal of DGNPME for better monitoring. The reply was not acceptable as
Procurement Cell within SPMU was to act as a professional unit to manage procurements and
also DGNPME had failed to effectively monitor the procurements which suffered from
delays, high costs and non/partial supplies.

6.2 Procurement
6.2.1 Planning

The State and Districts were to have a procurement and logistics plan. Instances of inability
to procure and non-utilisation of procured items were observed due to lack of proper
planning. This defeated the objective of timely procurement and resulted in blockage of
funds.

6.2.1.1 Procurement of EMTS

To reduce delays in access to medical care during emergencies, SHS planned for Emergency
Medical Transport Services (EMTS) during 2010-11 throughout the State, with 988
ambulances for 71.48 crore. EMTS was to be operated in PPP mode. DGNPME issued
supply order (July 2010) to TATA Motors for 988 vehicles at DGS&D rates for
र₹ 70.04 crore. The delivery was to be made between October 2010 and February 2011 as per
monthly schedule. DGNPME had paid % 42.51 crore to the firm for supply of 589 vehicles.
Out of 589, DGNPME had physically taken over only 159 vehicles and sent them
(November 2010) to the districts. The firm supplied all remaining vehicles against stated
revised supply order of 779 vehicles, and they were parked in the firm’s premises.

- Vehicles parked in vendor’s premises, Lucknow, 01 November 2011.

deterioration of asset lying in the open.

6.2.1.2 Inability to procure Medicines and Equipment

EC approved (2010-11) and released % 37.26 crore to DGNPME for procurement of
medicines and equipment like IFA & De-worming tablets, Kits for ASHA, Pregnancy test
kits, new drug kits, child survival kits etc. However, it was observed that DGNPME
surrendered (23 April 2011) % 26.40 crore to SPMU and transferred % 10.86 crore to districts
during 2010-11 because of its inability to make purchases. The Government stated that
tendering process was abandoned due to higher rates of certain items and the Government’s
intimation that financial power of DGNPME was limited to हैं 10 lakh. The reply was not
acceptable as financial powers of DGNPME were well-known and the reasonability of price
was not ensured.

6.2.2 Contract management

Audit observed that contract management was weak. This resulted in non-protection of the
interests of the stakeholders, extension of undue benefits to the vendors, delay in supply and
blockage of funds.

6.2.2.1 Procurements through UNOPS

EC decided (July 2008) to hire United Nations Offices for Project Services (UNOPS) as its
procurement agent in view of non-formation of Procurement Cell within SPMU. UNOPS was
selected as it felt that UNOPS was a professional procurement agency with specialisation in
medical supplies and Ministry of Health and Family Welfare in Gol had hired for its
procurements under World Bank funded project. SHS entered (July 2008) into contract with

Performance Audit Report on National Rural Health Mission for the year ended 31 March 2011 | 97


Procurement - State Level

= without protecting its own interests. The unfavorable clauses included 100 per cent
advance payment on finalisation of tender, refund clause of advance on simple interest
without bank/performance guarantee and absence of punitive clauses for failure to comply
with contract obligations, including liquidated damages clause. SHS was to be associated in
the whole procurement process as an observer. During the bid evaluation process its role as
an observer was limited to “concurrence with recommendations limited to pricing only”.
Audit observed that there was a lack of technical expertise in SPMU to carry out the tasks
and it did not participate in bid evaluation process of high value items. Further, no reports and
returns, although stipulated in agreement were received/called for. No proper monitoring
system was established, resulting in delayed supplies, non-supply of certain items and
blockage of funds.

The Government in its reply stated that the agreement to engage UNOPS as procurement
agent for SHS was identical to a similar agreement entered into by the Ministry of Health &
Family Welfare, Gol. However, Audit observed that the provisions related to World Bank
funded procurements and these were not applicable to NRHM, which envisaged open and
competitive system for procurement.

6.2.2.2 Blockage of funds by UNOPS

SHS and DGNPME together placed two procurement requests with UNOPS. One in July
2008 for हैं 74.29 crore and another in January, 2010 for = 81.04 crore. With respect to supply
order for Medicine and equipment, placed in July 2008, UNOPS supplied 724 laparoscopes
for = 51.36 crore with a delay of 223 days. Similarly, for procurement requests placed in
January 2010, it took eight months (October 2010) for part supply (¥ 39.81 crore) from the
date of payment of advance (March 2010). Balance supplies worth हैं 41.23 crore were not
made till December 2010. After persuasion and filing of a FIR in November 2010, UNOPS
refunded हें 38.90 crore in December 2010. An amount of & 2.33 crore remains unrecovered
(December 2011).

Audit also observed that an advance of हैं 81.04 crore made to UNOPS in
March, 2010, included items worth = 19.08 crore which were not approved in PIP (2009-10).
This was included by diversion of fund from other activities (Appendix-6.1).

Thus, SHS and DGNPME extended undue favours to UNOPS including award of large
supply orders by diverting ¥ 19.08 crore from other activities. It also failed to manage the
contract with the supplier, which led to erratic supply, blockade of funds and unrecovered
advance.

The Government admitted that supply of laparoscope and zinc sulphate tablets was delayed
and attributed it to its inability to provide road permits to the supplier firm. It also denied
diversion citing approved RoP (approved PIP for 2009-10) which was factually incorrect.

6.2.2.3. Award of work by UNOPS

UNOPS as procurement agent was to manage tendering process in an open, fair and
transparent manner. Audit examined the system of tendering by UNOPS through a case study
relating to procurement of laparoscopes amounting to हैं 51.38 crore. UNOPS invited tenders
for 1224 laparoscopes in five Schedules to enable suppliers to bid according to their capacity
(Appendix-6.2) in August 2008. Four suppliers participated in the tender. Records relating to

98 | Performance Audit Report on National Rural Health Mission for the year ended 31 March 2011


Procurement - State Level

= Il showed that one bidder, M/s Pregna International, which was assessed as L-1
bidder, was not awarded the work on the ground that it was not technically compliant.
Scrutiny of Bid Evaluation Report showed that UNOPS followed three stage evaluations.
Initially, a preliminary and general assessment was performed to verify completeness of bids
and to review suppliers’ background. All bids found to be substantially responsive in this
assessment were taken up for detailed price and technical evaluation. Thus, technical
evaluation was done with full knowledge of financial bids.

Audit observed that:

a) Though the Government claimed that Bid Evaluation Committee (BEC) comprised
three members in which two were independent and in no way connected with UNOPS,
the fact was that BEC was chaired by Senior Pharmaceutical Product/ Supply Chain
Officer, UNOPS and the other member was Financial Consultant, UNOPS;

b) Though the Government claimed that technical evaluation was done by a very senior
and eminent expert from AIIMS, the fact was that the technical assessment sheet was
signed only by two UNOPS members and not by third technical expert from AIIMS;

c) The system of technical assessment after the price evaluation of substantially
responsive bids was against Gol/CVC° and GoUP guidelines and best practices and was
also fraught with risk of fraud and corruption. SHS did not insist on two-envelope
system which was very much permissible under UNOPS procurement manual.

d) Under Standards and warranty provision of technical assessment report, the product of
M/s Pregna International was found to be in conformity with industry standards®.
However, in final technical assessment the firm was disqualified on the ground that the
firm did not provide evidence of CE certification. This was done after BEC noted that
firm claimed ISO certificate issued by Quality Systems (QS) certification services to be
in line with CE requirement;

e) Post qualification assessment sheet stated that the qualified bidder had valid ISO-9001
and ISO-13485/13488 standards. There was no mention of compliance of product with
FDA, CE, Underwriters Laboratories Inc. (UL) or BIS standards;

f) Against estimated cost of laparoscope of % 4 lakh per unit in PIP of 2007-08 SHS
accepted the offer of second lowest and qualified bidder Karl Storz at = 6.55 lakh per
unit. This was done knowing that M/s Pregna International’s offer was हैं 3.94 lakh and
SHS could exercise its power of “concurrence with recommendation limited to pricing
only” to reject Karl Storz offer. On further inquiry UNOPS itself admitted that Tamil
Nadu had procured laparoscopes at हैं. 4.67 lakh per unit. UNOPS, however, justified
higher cost due to richer specifications (¥ 0.45 lakh), customs duty, incidental costs
(₹ 0.53 lakh) and exchange variations; and

g) M/s Pregna International Limited, complained to Mission Director about irregularities
in tender procedure adopted by UNOPS and an internal inquiry was held. Since Mission

6.2.2.4 Supply of Intrauterine Device (IUD) Kits

PIP of 2007-08 included provision for Daily [UD 380-A counseling service for achieving the
objective of Population Stabilisation. This service was to be provided in different District
Women Hospitals, 426 CHCs and 397 block PHCs under NRHM Additionalities. 5000 IUD
Kits were to be procured for counseling service. No procurement was, however, made during
2007-08. Procurement of these kits was undertaken during 2008-09 in two schedules (stages)
through UNOPS. In each schedule, 2500 IUD Kits were to be supplied.

UNOPS invited bids (September 2008) for procurement of 5000 IUD Kits. Two bids were
received. The lowest bidder was technically disqualified. The other bidder, M/s South India
Surgical Company Limited was awarded the contract. The rates quoted by M/s South India
Surgicals Co. Limited, were higher than the rate of = 3000 per kit provided in PIP. Therefore,
the accepted bid of = 4844 per kit exceeded the rate given in PIP by = 1844 (61 per cent). The
tendering process which was reduced to a single bid and the quoted price being higher than
the reserve price warranted re-tendering.

Against an allocation of हैं 150 lakh in the approved PIP, UNOPS received % 242.19 lakh
from SPMU (November 2008) as advance for the supply of IUD kits. Receipt and
distribution of these kits to hospitals was not confirmed by SPMU and the approval for the
excess expenditure of हैं 92.19 lakh was also not obtained.

The Government stated that a single bid situation did not necessarily warrant re-tendering as
per UNOPS procurement procedures. The Government, thus, followed the policies of
procurement agents without protecting its own interest, thereby depriving the State of the
benefits of competitive rates.

6.2.2.6 Mobile Medical units (MMUs)

It was proposed to operationalise 135 MMUs in 15 districts’ of the State. MMUs were to be
positioned at selected villages/habitation, as per a pre-determined regular monthly schedule.
One MMU was to be positioned in each block, except district headquarters’ block. Each
MMU was to work for eight hours a day, for six days a week. Thus, one MMU was to cover
approximately six centres/villages in a month.

The operationalisation of MMUs was to be done by private service providers under PPP
model. SHS/ DGNPME was to pay the cost of vehicle and fabrication to the manufacturers of
vehicles and fabricators through letters of credit (LC) while recurring cost for medicines,
management, IEC efc. was to be reimbursed to service providers on actual basis. A provision
of & 42.68 crore was made in PIP of 2010-11 for operation of MMUs as detailed below:

Procurement - State Level

= observed that:

. The Consultant was to prepare the bid document (Request for Proposal, RFP) for
operationalisation of MMU. However, DGNPME invited tender ten days before
selection of the Consultant;

न As per extant financial orders, DGNPME’s financial power was limited to € 10 lakh for
purchase of equipment. Moreover, procurement of service provider for operation of
MMU was complex and a new procurement system and, therefore, a Consultant was
hired. Still DGNPME accepted the composite tender of हैं 65.39 crore (inclusive of
capital cost of % 35 crore);

न The Government/SHS bore capital costs of vehicle and fabrication without ownership.
Further, they were to bear recurring costs (salaries of staff, fuel and vehicle
maintenance, consumables) on specified terms. Such an arrangement imposed
minimum risk on service provider and all the risk got transferred to the
Government/SHS. Despite this DGNPME stated (October 2011) that it was a contract
agreement and not a PPP mode procurement.

. DGNPME had contracted service providers at rates higher than those sanctioned in PIP.
The capital costs went up from हें 17.93 to € 35.04 crore (95 per cent) while operational
cost went up from % 24.30 crore to हैं 30.35 crore (25 per cent) per year as shown in
Table -6.3. Thus, DGNPME did not ensure execution of the scheme within the
sanctioned amount in PIP and incurred excess expenditure of = 18.11 crore as capital
cost;

° DGNPME changed the contract condition of making direct payments to fabricators
through LC. It opened LC in service provider’s name for making payments to the
fabricators. This change allowed service provider to get fabrication done at lower rates
and get undue benefit;

न DGNPME did not give brand name or make of vehicles and specific design for
fabrication while calling for RFPs. This resulted in wide variations in prices. For
instance, the ratio of cost of vehicle to cost of fabrication varied from 2.22 to 5.38.

. Integrity and confidentiality of the tendering process was not ensured. RFP required
submission of financial and technical bids in soft copy. However, many CDs furnished
by the bidders contained both financial and technical bids. Thus, separate secrecy of
technical and financial bids was not ensured;

. There were delays in operation of MMUs by service providers which attracted penalty
but it was not calculated, imposed and recovered. The exact dates of operationalisation
of MMUs were not furnished to Audit;

. During physical verification of MMUs by the Transport Commissioner, it was found
that in many cases many items of fabrication and equipment were missing. Thus, the
department failed to ensure fabrication and installation of equipment in MMUs as per
specifications. Moreover, no effective action was taken or systemic improvements made
as follow up to the enquiry reports of Transport Commissioner and Chairman, Board of

report of the Chairman, Board of Revenue. However, Audit observed that DGNPME had
furnished (August 2011) its response to findings of Chairperson, Board of Revenue in July
2011.

6.2.2.7. Procurement of FRU Kits

EC sanctioned and released 31.59 crore* to DGNPME for purchase of FRU/IUD kits. The
High Power Purchase Committee, headed by Principal Secretary, decided (19 August 2009)
to purchase these items through UPSIC, on the ground that GO dated 01 July 2000 provided
for purchase of family planning kits and surgical items from UPSIC. The rates were to be
decided on the basis of tenders to be invited by UPSIC. DGNPME placed (28 August, 2009)
the supply order on UPSIC for the supply of 515 FRU kits and 2050 IUD kits. DGNPME
entered into an agreement with UPSIC on terms and conditions, which were not legally
vetted and approved by competent authority. This resulted in the incorporation of a condition
that 90 per cent advance payment would be made to UPSIC which was also against past
practices adopted by DGNPME where only 25 per cent advance was admissible to the
nominated agencies.

According to the agreement (3 September, 2009) between DGNPME and UPSIC, the
equipment, with ISI certification and CE mark, were to be supplied within 90 days. The
company, selected for supply by UPSIC, was to fulfil WHO/GMP” standards. In case of
delay, a penalty of हैं 1000 per day was to be imposed. A representative of DGNPME was to
be part of the tendering process. UPSIC was to keep DGNPME informed about the progress
of the tendering process.

8 9/2009 : ₹ 28.62 crore and 12/2009 : ₹ 2.97 crore,
° World Health Organisation (WHO) and Good Manufacturing Practices (GMP).

104 | Performance Audit Report on National Rural Health Mission for the year ended 31 March 2011


Procurement - State Level

= items of medical instruments had been reserved for procurement from MSME (Micro,
Small & Medium Enterprises). UPSIC, therefore, sought procurement of these items from the
firms registered as MSME. A short term tender notice of 13 days for procurement of
FRU/IUD kits was invited. Orders for supply of 515 FRU Kits at the rate of 5.94 lakh per
kit and 2050 IUD Kits at the rate of हैं 4859 per kit were placed on Surgicoin Mediquip (P)
Ltd and agreement was signed with the party. The party supplied the kits (FRU Kits: 515,
IUD Kits: 2050) and payment of = 31.59 crore was released to the party.

As per terms and conditions (Clause 30) of the technical bid), the firms submitting their offer
must have valid WHO, GMP certification. However, the manufacturing facility of Surgicoin
Mediquip (P) Limited, registered with UPSIC from where the supplies were made, was not
having WHO, GMP certification. As per clause 3(e) of the technical bid, the firms submitting
their offer must enclose their commercial tax registration under VAT, including CST, along
with their offer. However, the supplier had been registered with VAT Authorities on
29 September 2009 i.e. after the date of opening of the tender. Therefore, the supplier in
question did not technically qualify to participate in the bid and as such its bid was not valid
and, therefore, not to be considered.

The firm applied for registration on 29 August 2009 and was registered on the same day in
UPSIC for participation in UPSIC’s assistance scheme just one day (28 August 2009) after
the supply order was placed on UPSIC by DGNPME.

M/s SGS Private Limited, Ghaziabad, was selected by UPSIC for third party inspection.
Scope of inspection and testing covered quality inspection, conformity to specifications,
performance test, quality verification and review of Test Certificate. Certificates given by
M/s SGS (P) Ltd. for pre-dispatch material revealed that it did not cover performance test
results against the six parameters. Report submitted by the third party inspectors, did not
show tests with reference to relevant scope of inspection and testing including performance
check.

The Government instead of replying to audit observation stated that UPSIC would respond.
However, UPSIC did not furnish any reply to this and other audit observations relating to
procurements mentioned in this Report.

that:

a) Despite the fact that the then Secretary/Mission Director had directed (February 2007)
not to procure computers from M/s Shreetron India Limited for reasons of supply on

= quality of computer. The supplier was chosen by EC despite availability of

6.2.2.10 Deficiencies in hiring of vehicles by SPMU

The State Project Management Unit (SPMU) did not invite proper tenders for hiring of
vehicles for the headquarters office at Lucknow to avail the benefit of competitive rates. It
also violated the approved pre-bid conditions in considering the offers received. SPMU not
only incurred higher cost on hiring but also allowed operation of non-commercial vehicles
for the purpose.

The work was awarded (26 August, 2008) to Singh Travel Agency, Lucknow (Agency),
selected on short term tender basis for a period of two years. SPMU paid (2009-11)
1.01 crore to the Agency as hiring charges. Audit observed the following deficiencies in
availing the services of hired vehicle:

(a) Relaxation of pre-bid criteria

The pre-bid criteria approved (19 June, 2008) by the Mission Director included participation
by a registered travel agency, registered with Service Tax Department and having authorised

1 0.0. 08/78-IT-2-2001 dated 12September, 2001; G.O. 785/78-2-2004-25-IT/2004 dated 25 August, 2004

Performance Audit Report on National Rural Health Mission for the year ended 31 March 2011 | 107


Procurement - State Level

= vehicles. The criteria also provided agencies to possess a minimum of 15 cars
(mainly Indica, Ambassador, Indigo, Esteem or other semi-luxury) of 2005 or later makes.
Notwithstanding the approved pre-bid criteria, the criteria were relaxed lowering the
possession of taxis from 15 to 3 and changing the year of the make from 2005 to 2000.

As the Agency had only six vehicles (three not registered as taxi) and cars of years earlier
than 2005, the relaxation facilitated the Agency to participate in the tender.

(b) Excess expenditure on hiring

During the same period, another sister unit located in the same complex viz. State Innovations
in Family Planning Services Project Agency (SIFPSA), was also hiring vehicles from the
same agency at हैं 20519 per month per AC taxi during the same period. Against this, SPMU
allowed higher rate of ¥ 23500 to the same Agency. Further, the rates obtained from two
other agencies” against the short term tender, rejected on untenable grounds, were
₹ 18275 and हैं 18990 respectively. Compared to the rates of the sister unit (® 20519), the
excess expenditure on hiring of vehicles worked out to = 9.30 lakh for two years. Further, the
service tax registration number of the service provider was also not mentioned on the bills of
the Agency, as contemplated in Gol circular of August 2007 on Service Tax, facilitating
evasion of service tax.

(c) Private cars used for commercial purposes

Further, a list of 35 vehicles cross verified from the RTO, Lucknow indicated that (i) only
two vehicles (out of 35 vehicles) were registered as taxis; (ii) registration numbers mentioned
were incomplete/incorrect in respect of 20 vehicles and (iii) out of 13 remaining vehicles,
only five vehicles were for commercial use. The rest eight were private vehicles.

SPMU stated (November 2011) that the Agency had been asked to put their service tax
registration no. on all their bills. For violating the pre-bid criteria, it stated that approval for
the same could not be obtained due to time constraint and that a letter was being sent to the
agency to provide renewed registration numbers of its vehicles.

The reply was indicative of SPMU’s failure to adhere to the prescribed procedures/
Government Rules and disregard to the directives of higher authorities.

= Supply of Incomplete ASHA Kits

A provision of = 6.75 crore was made in PIP of 2008-09 for supply of 1.35 lakh ASHA kits.

amount of % 375 for each kit. Resultantly, the quantity and items of kit were reduced
(Appendix-6.5). Thus, due to inability to procure IFA tablets at competitive rates, kits with
reduced quantity of medicines and without some items were made available to ASHAs.

SPMU did not reply to the core issue of competitive rates and elaborated on irregular supply
of Kit A and Kit B by Gol.

6.2.4 Approval of Requirements and Funding
6.2.4.1 Procurement of furniture and instruments

DGNPME entered (24 January 2011) into an agreement with LACCFED for supply of
furniture and instruments to 3867 accredited sub centres of the State under JSY. As per the
agreement, furniture and instruments of approved specifications were to be supplied within
two months. There was a penalty clause of 0.1 per cent per day on the cost of supplied
material for delays. Three-fourth of total sanctioned/estimated cost of = 10.80 crore was
given as advance. Items to be supplied included delivery beds, examination tables, footsteps
(single), weighing scales (adult), baby weighing scales, BP instruments, stoves, emergency
lanterns, hospital beds, mattresses, pillows, sheets, blankets etc.

Audit observed that:

a) LACCFED was awarded the work on nomination basis even though it was not a
recognised/empaneled agency for supply of medical store and furniture efc. Finance
Department had allowed (May 2010) provisional empanelment of LACCFED only for
construction work;

b) LACCFED did not go for competitive bidding. Quotations were called from six firms in
an arbitrary manner and without specifications. LACCFED agreed to undertake full
supplies for not less than हैं 14.83 crore against the estimated cost of % 10.93 crore.
DGNPME agreed to take the supplies at rates offered by LACCFED by reducing the
scope of work without examining its reasonableness;

c) The DGNPME did not seek the approval of EC for higher rates and procurements of
reduced quantity limiting the PIP amount to हैं 10.93 crore. Further, DGNPME did not
explore the possibility of buying the items on DGS&D, ESI or CMSD rate contracts;

d) Quality and specifications of supplied material was not checked/ensured by DGNPME
through third party evaluation, as per contract conditions;

e) Although supply was not completed (November 2011), the clause for penalty for delay
was not invoked against LACCFED.

Performance Audit Report on National Rural Health Mission for the year ended 31 March 2011 | 111


Procurement - State Level

= Government stated that the work was allotted to LACCFED as per decision of EC
(August 2010). DGNPME had directed LACCFED that material would be supplied as per
DGS&D rate contract or ESIC rates, in absence of which purchase would be done as per
Uttar Pradesh Government purchase policy. It was also directed (February 2011) that rates
given in PIP were the maximum permissible. Action was being taken for third party
evaluation. The Government assured that penalty for delay will be imposed at the time of
final payment.

The reply of the Government was not acceptable as the decision of awarding work without
tender to LACCFED for supply of hospital material was not justified. It was a nominated
agency for construction and not for supply of medical material. Although it was
communicated by DGNPME that rates given in PIP were the maximum permissible, rates
given by LACCFED were almost 1.5 times higher than those in the PIP.

6.2.4.2 Providing CUG connectivity

PIP of 2009-10 provided an allotment of = 76.80 lakh under Mission flexi pool for supplying
Closed User Group (CUG) connectivity to the doctors/ officers/ other related employees for
the whole year. This CUG was to connect 1000 users and the unit connectivity charge was
assessed at = 640per user per month. EC approved (August 2009) the proposal for obtaining
1000 CUG connections from M/s BSNL at the rate of हैं 640 per month. SPMU placed an
order (October 2009) on M/s BSNL, for 1250 connections.

The actual expenditure worked out to = 1.02 crore against € 76.80 lakh provided in PIP. This
led to unauthorised diversion of ¥ 25.20 lakh. Additional Connections, numbering 250, were
procured in excess of the numbers approved in PIP involving an unapproved expenditure of
र₹ 10.55 lakh. Handsets, numbering 28, were not distributed (November 2011). This indicated
that these were actually not required and an expenditure of € 1.91 lakh on their purchase was
avoidable. Audit further observed that the purchase included 10 Blackberry Bold 3G handsets
which were more expensive than the other GSM mobile handsets.

The Government stated that EC (August 2009) decided to procure maximum numbers of
handsets from BSNL in the given budget so that CUG network facility might be provided to
maximum number of functionaries. BSNL agreed to provide 1250 connections at the same
cost sanctioned for 1000 CUG connections.

The reply of the Government was not acceptable because as per the said EC meeting,
76.80 lakh was approved for 1000 users at the rate of हैं 640 per month for the year
2009-10. But BSNL provided 1250 CUG with recurring expenditure for only six months
instead of 12 months. Moreover, the reason for unauthorised excess expenditure of % 25.20
lakh was not provided by the Government.

It was observed that supplies were not as per the prescribed specifications and within the
specified time frame due to absence of monitoring.

= Installation of Reverse Osmosis (RO) systems and strengthening of toilets

Installation of 133 units of RO systems for हैं 19.95 crore was approved in PIP of 2009-10 to

recurring expenditure on the systems would be met through PPP mode. Further, हैं 2.68 crore
was provided in PIP for improving the toilet facilities at District Hospitals. As per the

even after two years of award of work, despite the fact that 90 per cent advance was
paid to UPSIC. By October 2011, only 120 RO systems were installed, whereas work of
strengthening of toilets had not yet been taken up. DGNPME did not make any efforts
to invoke the penalty clause; and

° The quality and specifications of RO systems were not got checked by DGNPME.
There were frequent demands for repairs by Chief Medical Suprintendents (CMSs) of
different hospitals. UPSIC had not submitted the inspection reports regarding
installation and functioning of plants. An internal inquiry set up by the Government
pointed to shortcomings in the supplies/installations (September 2011).

As pointed out earlier the Government did not respond on the plea that UPSIC would respond
to the audit observation. UPSIC, however, gave no reply.

6.2.5.3 Deficiencies in execution of contract for Hospital Waste Management

Uttar Pradesh Health System Development Project (UPHSDP), a World Bank Aided Project
in the State, entered into an agreement (August 2006) with four firms (M/s SNG Mercantile
Private Ltd., M/s Synergy Waste Management, M/s S. 5. Medical System, M/s Sonosite
Medical Systems (I) Pvt. Ltd.) for Hospital Waste Management in hospitals of the States on
turn-key basis. The scope of work included supply of equipment and consumables, recurring
training to concerned staff, waste collection/ transportation to their Combined Treatment
Facility (CTF), treatment/disinfection and finally disposal of biomedical waste as per
Biomedical Waste Management and Handling Rules,1998 and Central Pollution Control
Board’s (CPCB) guidelines.

As per the agreement (August 2006), 80 per cent payment to firms was to be made by
DGNPME on presentation of bill at the end of cach month after verification. However,
र₹ 1.73 crore was paid (May—June 2010) to two firms, M/s SS Medical system and M/s SS
Medical India Pvt. Ltd., without verification of bills. Remaining 20 per cent payment was to
be made after receipt of review committee report. No payment could be made to firms for
want of this report. Besides, as per the agreement, the firms were to submit registration
numbers and type of vehicles being used for carriage of waste and records of insurance and
EPF details of employees engaged. Only two firms, M/s Synergy Waste Management and
M/s SNG Mercantile, submitted such records. The two other firms, M/s SS Medical system
and M/s SS Medical System Pvt. Ltd., did not submit these records. Payment for 2010-11
was not made to firms, although budget of € 13 crore was available with DGNPME.

The Government in its reply stated that the remaining 20 per cent payment was not made in
view of non-formation of review committee.

6.2.5.4 Repair of Vehicles

For 2006-07, State Empowered Committee of RCH Programme approved release of
% 3.50 crore on (March 2007) for mobility support for monitoring and evaluation. DGNPME
released this amount @ % 5 lakh to 70 CMOs for repair of the Government vehicles and
deployment of contractual drivers to help intensive monitoring and evaluation of different
NRHM programmes.

DGNPME decided (May 2007) that five vehicles be repaired under each CMO and five
vehicle drivers may be appointed on contract for driving these vehicles in the absence of
regular drivers. Contractual drivers were to be appointed at the district level by forming a
committee. The monthly expenditure was to be submitted to DGNPME by the fifth of the
following month.

114 | Performance Audit Report on National Rural Health Mission for the year ended 31 March 2011


Procurement - State Level

= observed that neither any statement of expenditure nor any detail about the repair of
vehicles and deployment of contractual drivers were intimated (December 2011) to
DGNPME. List of repaired vehicles, their utilisation, expenditure on repair, operational
expenses on vehicles and contractual drivers engaged were not available with DGNPME.

The Government replied that it had written to the districts on 31 October 2011 calling for
information on expenditure of the fund. The fact remains that the Government was unaware
of utilisation of fund even after four years of its release.

6.2.5.5 Procurement of Deep Freezers

SHS engaged (August 2008) UNOPS to provide procurement services for purchase of 140
Deep Freezers (-10°C to -20°C) for 140 First Referral Units (FRUs) in 70 districts under
Immunisation programme. The estimated price of each unit was 50,000. SPMU advanced
(February 2009) ₹ 73.70 lakh, being the total cost of 140 Deep Freezers, to UNOPS.

The Deep Freezers supplied by UNOPS did not comply with the required specifications i.e.
FDA, CE (Certificate of Europe), UL or ISI (Indian Standard Institute) certified.

The Government stated that the supplier in this case submitted ‘CCC’ certificate which the
Bid Evaluation Committee, comprising of Technical Expert from the All India Institute of
Medical Sciences, New Delhi, found acceptable. The reply was not factually correct as the
Committee had irregularly relaxed this condition on different grounds and no ‘CCC’
certificate was furnished by the supplier.

6.2.5.6 Procurement of printed material for Filaria Day

Additional Director, Malaria and Vector Borne Diseases, Lucknow paid (March 2008)
र₹ 6.62 lakh to Sanyukta Samiti Udyog, Chinahat, Lucknow for Pamphlets, Folders and
Posters meant for Filaria Day observed from 25 to 27 February 2008. The bill numbers by
which payments were reportedly made were 2382, 2383 and 2384, all dated 25 February
2008. On verification from the Commercial Tax Department (2 November, 2011), these bills
were found cancelled in the sale statement submitted by the supplier firm. Given the
cancellation of the said sale from the sale bill, the payment appeared to be fraudulent.

6.2.5.7 Loss in printing of publicity material for healthcare

SPMU, Centre for Health Action, Research and Training (CHART) and DGNPME required
publicity material for healthcare to be used or distributed by the DHSs and other associated
offices. Audit observed that in violation of the guidelines and procurement rules, they did not
invite tenders for competitive rates. Instead, they placed 32 work orders valuing
₹ 16.55 crore’ between April 2008 and March 2011'* on UP Cooperative Federation Press,
Lucknow (Press) against which हैं 12.64 crore was paid (June 2008-January 2011) to the
Press”.

fraudulent transactions.

6.2.5.8 Telemedicine

A project for creating telemedicine facilities for rural people, to provide access to advance
medical facilities in ten districts of State, with support of Sanjay Gandhi Postgraduate
Institute of Medical Sciences (SGPGI), Lucknow, was approved in the State PIP for 2008-09
for ¥ 9.15 crore. In each selected district, specified health facilities (PHC, CHC, District
Hospital) were to be connected to a State level hospital (SGPGI). The districts were to be
selected on the basis of distance from tertiary care facilities, remoteness and inaccessibility to
specialised care either in the Government or the private sector.

The Government empanelled (July 2008) National Consumer Cooperative Federation of India
(NCCF), Lucknow, on nomination basis and released हैं 9.15 crore (June 2009). NCCF
established the telemedicine centres with video conferencing facilities at medical colleges of
Allahabad, Kanpur, Meerut, T.B. Sapru Hospital, Allahabad, and strengthened the hub at
SGPGI, Lucknow. Audit observed that the scope of work had been completely changed from
linking health facilities of 10 remote districts to SGPGI to linking of medical colleges in
major towns with SGPGI. This was done without the approval of EC.

On being pointed out, DGMET admitted (October 2011) that the facility of telemedicine was
not to be created in Medical Colleges. On receipt of instructions, NCCF had established the
telemedicine facility in Medical Colleges for which no objection was received from either the
Government or SPMU or DGMH. The reply was not convincing as there was no approval of
the competent authority, the EC.

Thus, the objective of providing telemedicine facilities for rural people of 10 districts was
defeated.

6.2.6.1 Idle Laparoscopes

Scrutiny of records of Logistic Management Cell (LMC), Lucknow, revealed that 1225 out of
1293 laparoscopes procured (2009-10: 724 & 2010-11: 569) from UNOPS were issued.
Sixty-Eight laparoscopes, amounting ~ 5.30 crore, were lying idle at LMC and
227 laparoscopes, amounting 0 = 15.89 crore, were lying unutilised in stores at 23 test
checked districts (Appendix-6.7).

DGNPME stated (November 2011) that laparoscopes had been kept in reserve for
replacement in various training facilities in case of break down and also for utilisation in
mega camps. Hence, it was sort of backup arrangement in case of contingencies. The reply
was not acceptable as PIP or any other procurement plan did not mention such intended use.
Further, the Government furnished no reply about laparoscopes lying idle at district levels.

6.2.6.2 Stores management

It was observed from the Stock Registers of logistic management cell (LMC) that UNOPS
had supplied (August 2010) 52.10 crore IFA tablets at the rate of % 14 per 100 tablets for
र₹ 6.61 crore. LMC received the consignments without physical verification and quality

Performance Audit Report on National Rural Health Mission for the year ended 31 March 2011 | 117


Procurement - State Level

= Further, LMC issued these IFA tablets to different CMOs through suppliers. The
receipt of medicine in CMO’s store was to be cross-checked with reference to an
acknowledgement with stock entry. The acknowledgement in the form of counterfoil was to
be signed by the store in-charge and countersigned by the concerned CMO. Audit, however,
observed that all CMOs had not furnished this acknowledgement, and, wherever furnished
these were not countersigned by the concerned CMOs. This raised doubts over supplies. As
per financial rules, physical verification of all stores should be made at least once every year.
The physical verification of stores had not been done.

DGNPME stated that the acknowledgement vouchers had been called for from concerned
CMOs. The Government did not furnish any reply.

6.3 Recommendations:

. SHS should strengthen its procurement system through establishment of Procurement
and logistics wing, laying down of Procurement Manual and preparation of annual
procurement plan;

° Procurement related risks should be periodically identified and reviewed to mitigate
risks in procurement. Adequate and effective controls should be put in place.
Complexities in Public Private Partnerships (PPP) should be managed in a
professional manner;

e — Procurement through open, transparent and competitive system should be resorted to
get value for money; and

° Expeditious action on unutilized assets should be taken.

= Training for VHASC members

As per decision of EC (December 2009), training for VHSC members was to be organised at

out of हैं 8.40 crore released to them for training VHSC members under Awareness

CMO, FW in violation of orders of the DM, transferred these funds to MOICs on 22 March

(DPM), District Account Manager (DAM) and District Community Mobiliser (DCM) did not

of funds (23 March 2011) and despite DM’s order of 8 March 2011 to hold a meeting on
modalities of conducting this training. CMO, Agra offered no comments on the audit
observation.

7.2 Expenditure under Untied funds

Untied funds and AMG are given to CHCs, BPHCs, APHCs, sub centres and VHSCs in
specified amounts every year. ECs of RKSs at CHCs/PHCs accord financial sanctions, after
ascertaining needs through Advisory Committees. Similarly, at sub centre and VHSC levels,
Gram Pradhan and ANM undertake financial transactions after need/requirements are
ascertained through VHSC.

The Government ordered (October 2010) that CMO, FW would be joint signatory for
transactions at the block level, citing large number of transactions and limited accounting
knowledge at that level. Test check of records revealed that this decision was taken on the
proposal of DGNPME. The proposal was not examined by SPMU or EC of SHS, who were
responsible for financial management of NRHM funds. The Government did not consider
strengthening the Block Programme Management Units (BPMU) as an option to strengthen
financial management at the block level, despite Gol’s repeated suggestions to this effect.
Following reporting of financial irregularities in NRHM in April 2011, the Government had
created (June 2011) a post of Finance and Accounts officer, to be drawn from UP Finance
and Accounts Services, in DHS. The Government also abolished (April 2011) the post of
CMO, FW and the system of joint signatory referred to above.

Test check of records revealed that introduction of system of joint operation of block level
accounts in October 2010 was followed by occurrence of financial irregularities.
7.2.1 Withholding of Untied funds at block level

In order to decentralise decision making and ensure community participation, RKSs were
created as a separate legal entity to exercise administrative and financial powers. Similarly,

agra, Bahraich, Bareilly, Bulandshahar, Jalaun, Jaunpur, Jhansi, Kushi Nagar, Moradabad, Raebareli, Shahjahanpur and Unnao,

7.5 Other observations in sampled districts
7.5.1 Procurement ignoring rate contract

In order to effect economy, material should be purchased from the lowest bidder. However,
test check of records revealed that in the following cases material were purchased at rates
higher than those of Rate Contract (RC) of DGS&D and GoUP or without following due
procedure:

. CMO, Allahabad purchased (2010-11) Ciprofloxacin tablets valuing = 3.91 lakh at
₹ 18.60 per ten tablets, as per price given in RC of Employees State Insurance
Corporation (ESIC) although it was available at cheaper rate in the departmental RC at
रू 9.05 per ten tablets, thus, incurring an extra expenditure of हैं 2.01 lakh. The
Government replied that to avoid any hindrance in family welfare camps, the medicines
were procured on ESIC RC. The reply was not convincing as drugs were purchased at
higher rate despite the option of cheaper rate in the departmental RC.

न. CMO, Allahabad purchased (2010-11) medicines for = 1.90 lakh at higher rate on local
purchase despite option of cheaper rate in departmental RC. This resulted in extra
expenditure of हैं 1.37 lakh. Government replied that to avoid any hindrance in

124 | Performance Audit Report on National Rural Health Mission for the year ended 31 March 2011


Procurement - District Level

= of programmes, preference was given to local firms. The reply was not
convincing as cheaper option was not exercised.

CMO, Bareilly procured (2009-10) 35,000 IUD Kits by splitting the purchase. The kits
were purchased from M/s Charisma Health Care, Lucknow at the rate of & 20.06 per kit
including taxes against DGS&D rate of 10.53. This resulted in extra expenditure of
र₹ 3.34 lakh. In September 2010, various kits (RCH Follow-up Kit, RCH Camp Kit,
RCH Camp Surgical Kit and [UD Kit) worth = 12.36 lakh, were procured on quotation
basis. These kits contained general medicines/drugs which were available on DGS&D
RC list/CMSD RC list/RC approved by GoUP. The procurement of these kits at higher
rate, flouting procurement procedure, resulted in undue favour to the firm
(M/s Medicare, Lucknow) and loss of € 7.57 lakh to the Government. The Government
stated that complete kit was not available in RC of DGS&D, medicines given in the kits
were of better quality than the medicines available on RC; kits were also inclusive of
cost of packing and printing charges of pamphlets describing method of medicine
usage. The reply of the Government was not acceptable as the rates at which
procurements were made were higher by 90 per cent for purchases of 2009-10 and
158 per cent for purchases of September 2010, as compared to RC rates.

MOIC of PHC Bhatani, Deoria procured items like BP Machine, Emergency light,
needle destroyer and stethoscope from Untied fund, AMG and RKS funds. No
quotation/tender was invited. M/s Seva Enterprises supplied the items at rates higher
than printed MRP. Fictitious stock entries were made and funds were drawn but kept
undelivered as bank drafts. The Government stated that the quality of material was not
good, so the firm was not paid. The reply was contrary to the reply of MOIC during
audit when the facts were admitted. Further, no record of correspondence with the

were purchased (2010-11) from M/s S.K.P. Pharma, Lucknow at हैं 55 per kit, although
one kit could have been assembled at a cost of हैं 23.92, inclusive five per cent VAT, in
case medicines were purchased at rate contract. The Government replied that in view of
shortage of time and importance of the programme, purchases were made on quotation
basis as no RC rates were available for the kits. However, Audit observed that procured
cost was more than two times higher than the cost of the medicines in the Rate
Contract.

CMO, FW, Shahjahanpur purchased (December 2010) IUD Kits (₹ 6.37 lakh), on

of comparative analysis of rates, it was observed that the kits were procured at rates

= Deficiencies in procurement process

For ensuring quality of material purchased and obtaining competitive rates, it is necessary for
indenting authority to give wide publicity to the notice inviting tenders. It is also necessary
that the details of supplier/ vendor are registered with relevant tax authorities. Test check of
records revealed that this was not ensured as below:

. CMO, Jhansi made purchases (2008-11) from vendors not registered with different tax
authorities. The Government accepted the facts.

न. CMO, Raebareli, purchased (March 2011) IFA tablets through short term tender, from
M/s R.T.G. Pharmaceuticals, Lucknow, a firm technically ineligible as its Drug license
had expired on 31 December 2001 and the registration of Sales Tax/VAT expired on
31 March, 2006. Government replied that Drug licence was renewed till 31 December,
2011. The reply was not acceptable as, on the date of procurement, the firm had no
valid drug licence. Further, the Government stated that the firm had obtained permanent
registration with the Sales Tax department. No documentary evidence was produced
with the reply.

न. CMO, Varanasi purchased medicines worth हैं 23.78 lakh (RCH kits) during 2007-08
from UP Consumers Cooperative Federation Limited. According to the Government,
the renewal of Drug licence was applied for the period till 31 December 2011. The
reply was not correct as on the date of purchase, the Federation did not have
authorisation to sell medicines.

7.5.6 Procurement without requirements

At the time of purchase, the indenting authority should assess the actual requirement on the
basis of indents received from sources for whom the purchases were being made. Test check
revealed that equipment costing more than & 5.82 crore’ were lying unutilised.

. CMO Allahabad received (October-December 2010) 55 items from CSMD, Lucknow
neither against demand from the district nor as per provisions in District PIPs. Many of
the items Deep Freezer: 06 (₹ 3.16 lakh), Ice Lined Refrigerator (ILR), small: 07 and
large: 02 were lying unused (November 2011). Reply was not furnished by the
Government.

न. Three out of seven FRU Kits (₹ 17.82 lakh) received (October to December 2010) from
Lucknow for FRUs were not utilised as there are only four FRUs (Handia, Phoolpur,
Soraon and Karchhana) in Allahabad. The Government replied that all seven kits were
distributed to CHCs/PHCs. Reply was in contravention of the provisions of PIP which
did not provide for FRU kits to non-FRU units.

. CMO, Ballia received (June 2010) four solar refrigerators from Gol to maintain cold
chain services round the clock. As per insurance amount, cost of these refrigerators was
र₹ 5.25 crore. These were sent to four PHCs (Bahuri, Maniyar, Murli Chhapra and
Ratsar) in July 2010. Out of these, it was installed only at Bahuri but was not
functioning due to some defects. The Government accepted the facts.

न CMO, Ballia received deep freezers (Six MF 114 and two 286 - ₹ 3.16 lakh),
20 stabilizers and eight ILRs, for CHCs/PHCs and other hospitals. These were lying

unutilised (November 2011). The reply furnished by the Government was not relevant
as the procurement was against NRHM framework.

Audit observed that separate stock registers were maintained for 2010-11 by CMO FW
Agra, Etah and Jalaun instead of this being maintained by store keeper, under the
supervision of Senior Medical Officer.

Recommendations:

Procurements may be made only after assessing the requirements and the capacity to
utilise;

Procurements may be made on rate contract rates, wherever such rates are available.
In cases such rates are not available, due procedure in calling for quotations/tenders
and obtaining of sanctions from the competent authority may be strictly adhered to;

Procurements may be made against clearly defined specifications. Procured items may
be duly checked by competent authority against the prescribed specifications and due
entries be made in the stock register; and

Issue/utilisation of procured items may be duly entered in the stock/issue register which
should be periodically closed and reconciled to ensure effective logistic management.

Performance Audit Report on National Rural Health Mission for the year ended 31 March 2011 | 133


= Family Welfare, Reproductive

8 गाव chid Health

The Mission objectives included reduction in child and maternal mortality, population
stabilisation, gender and demographic balance. Universal access to public services for food
and nutrition, sanitation and hygiene, public health care services with emphasis on women’s
and children’s health and universal immunisation.

Some of the components of the Mission and their performance were as follows:

8.1 Janani Suraksha Yojana

The Janani Suraksha Yojana (JSY) was introduced in 2005-06 as a key intervention to enable
women to access institutional deliveries and thereby effect reductions in Maternal Mortality
Rate (MMR) and Infant Mortality Rate (IMR) to 100/100000 and 30/1000 live births
respectively by 2012. The website of MoHFW puts the levels (2009-10) of MMR and IMR in
Uttar Pradesh at 359/100000 and 63/1000 live births.

Institutional deliveries help pregnant women access skilled birth attendants more readily and
reliably and also improve their access to emergency obstetric care. JSY was to encourage
institutional deliveries by providing a financial package to all pregnant women.

All pregnant women in rural areas of high focus States, who deliver in a health centre, ware
eligible for a cash incentive of = 1,400 to meet both direct and indirect expenses incurred on
delivery. BPL women who deliver at home were also eligible for a cash incentive of € 500.

ASHAs have been given a major role in the implementation of the scheme. Each ASHA is
given हैं 200 to motivate a pregnant woman for institutional delivery and antenatal care and
paid हैं 250 for transport of a pregnant woman to the hospital. In addition, if the ASHA escorts
and stays in the health centre with the woman she is paid = 150. Thus, an ASHA is entitled to
receive हैं 600 if she performs all the above activities of motivation, transportation and
escorting a pregnant woman to the hospital.

8.1.1 Target and Achievement

The objective of the scheme was to achieve the target of 70 per cent institutional deliveries
by the end of March 2012. The target and achievement of institutional deliveries and cash
compensation paid under JSY during the period of the review were as under:

8.1.2 Implementation of the Scheme

Under NRHM framework all registered pregnant women were to be provided with Mother
and Child Health (MCH) Cards. ANMs were required to prepare Micro Birth Plan (MBP) for
identification of beneficiaries and monitoring of the antenatal and post natal care effectively.
In 217 test checked districts, Micro Birth Plan had not been prepared at PHCs and Sub centre
level. Consequently, institutional mechanism to track the registered pregnant women in the
test checked districts for checkups, institutional delivery and post delivery care by ANMs was
insufficient. Further, a majority of pregnant women, registered between 2005-06 and
2010-11, did not opt for treatment in health centres for institutional delivery. Of 71.18 lakh
institutional deliveries reported during 2005-11, 69.10 lakh pregnant women were provided
the incentive under JSY. Test check of records revealed that during 2006-11, an amount of
र₹ 5.71 lakh to be paid to beneficiaries for 434 institutional deliveries in four districts was not
paid as stated below:

8.1.3. Verification and monitoring

An amount of % 1,219.53 crore was disbursed towards cash compensation to 69.10 lakh
women for institutional deliveries during 2005-11. As per State PIP of 2008-09, 10 per cent
of JSY cases were to be verified. Further, in the year 2009-10, it was decided to ensure
monitoring of 5 to 10 per cent cases through deployment of monitors, departmental staff, as
well as through external agencies. 71 districts were to be covered in each quarter by
deploying four monitors, who would cover six to seven districts every month. These monitors
were to visit the districts to monitor JSY activities and follow-up complaints. Verification and
monitoring, as planned, was not conducted by SHS. Thus, expenditure of = 1,085.76 crore,
incurred under JSY, in 2008-09, 2009-10 and 2010-11 remained unverified.

The Government accepted the observation.

Test check of records of 22 out of 23 sampled districts revealed irregularities and fraudulent
payments as brought out below:

8.1.3.1 Excess payment

According to guidelines for implementation of JSY, if an institutional delivery of a rural
beneficiary takes place in an urban area hospital, she would be paid = 1400 on production of
proof of rural residential address like Kisan bahi, Kutumb register, Khasra khatauni, referral
slip submitted by ANM/ASHA or domicile certificate issued by Gram Pradhan. Otherwise,
she would be paid only & 1,000, as an urban beneficiary.

Performance Audit Report on National Rural Health Mission for the year ended 31 March 2011 | 137


Family Welfare, Reproductive and Child Health Welfare, Reproductive and Child Health

= four test checked District Hospitals (Female) during 2007-11, out of 61014 beneficiaries,
5608 beneficiaries (9.20 per cent) were paid ~ 1400, without obtaining proof of rural
residence as specified in the guidelines. As a result, the authenticity of payment of
% 22.43 lakh could not be established in audit (Appendix-8.2).

The Government in the case of DH(F), Mirzapur stated that incentive was paid on the basis of
referral slip but no documents to substantiate it were furnished to audit. In the case of DH(F),
Bulandshahar, the Government stated that incentive was paid on the basis of address proof of
husband. The reply was not acceptable as address of husband was not supported by proper
documents. In the case of DH(F), Khurja, Bulandshahar, the Government stated that incentive
of = 1400 was paid on the basis of verification of rural address by ASHA. The reply was not
acceptable because verification of address by ASHA was not a valid proof of residence.
Further, in the case of the Government Combined Hospital, Sikandarabad in district
Bulandshahar, the Government stated that instructions had been issued to ASHAs for
obtaining address proof from the beneficiaries.

8.1.3.2 Suspected Fraudulent payments

Test check of records revealed the following cases of payments of ~ 0.61 lakh, released
without proper documentary evidence highlighting the possibility of fraudulent payment of
र₹ 0.61 lakh:

. Between April 2009 and March 2011 payment was released by PHC, Kuandanda,
Bareilly to ASHAs for 36 rural institutional deliveries in CHC, Faridpur which were not
found entered in the delivery register. The payment made was calculated in audit as
% 0.22 lakh (at the rate of % 600 per delivery). The Government did not furnish any

reply.

. In PHC Mauaiama and Pratappur, Allahabad suspected payment of incentive of
0.13 lakh for nine deliveries during 2010-11 was pointed out (22 September 2011).
MOIC recovered (26 September 2011) हैं 0.09 lakh in 6 cases. The Government
accepted the audit observation.

. In PHC, Kuandanda, Bareilly, test check of claims in 128 cases, during 2009-11, made
by ASHAs as deliveries at District Hospital (Female), Bareilly revealed that
44 deliveries for which हैं 0.26 lakh was paid did not take place at the District Hospital
(Female), Bareilly.

The Government stated that ASHAs were paid incentive on the basis of beneficiaries’
discharge certificate, issued by DH (F) Bareilly. The reply was incorrect as CMS of the
hospital had certified that out of 128 deliveries, 44 had not taken place in DH(F) Bareilly.

8.1.3.3 Doubtful payments of incentive

. In District Hospital (Female), Budaun, 100 cases (in a bundle of vouchers starting from
09 August 2007) were test checked and it was observed that payment made to
beneficiaries as per slip issued to ASHA in 33 cases was shown at the rate of € 1000 per
beneficiary. However, as per vouchers (JSY card), the payment to the beneficiaries was
shown to be made at the rate of ¥ 1400 per beneficiary. The cash book was also altered
and % 400 was added to हैं 1000 to show payment at the rate of ¥ 1400. Thus, payment
of < 0.13 lakh appeared to be doubtful.

138 | Performance Audit Report on National Rural Health Mission for the year ended 31 March 2011


Family Welfare, Reproductive and Child Health, Welfare, Reproductive and Child Health

= Government did not furnish any reply.

. In PHC, Kuandanda, Bareilly, during 2007-08, 177 beneficiaries were paid incentive at
the rate of हैं 500 per case. But there was no proof of receipt, like signature, thumb
impression efc., of recipients in 172 cases. Thus, the payment of हैं 0.86 lakh was

The Government stated that beneficiaries were paid the incentive. But the documents in
support of the payments were stolen on 13 March 2011, for which FIR had been lodged.
However, details of the records stated to have been stolen were not recorded in the FIR.

8.1.3.4 Non-payment of transportation charges under JSY

The State PIP provided that in case ASHA fails to arrange transportation for a pregnant
woman to the hospital, the beneficiary would get = 250 immediately on reaching the hospital.
In four test checked units, it was observed that in 53,333 cases of delivery, the beneficiaries
came to hospital by arranging their own transport and were not paid transportation charges.
Thus, a payment of = 133.33 lakh, which was due to the beneficiaries, was not made during
2007-08 to 2010-11 (Appendix-8.3).

The Government did not give any reply in the case of DHS, Shahjahanpur. However, findings
of DHS, Jhansi were accepted. In the cases of DHSs Azamgarh and Deoria, it was stated that
the transportation charges were not paid as the beneficiaries did not submit the transportation
cost vouchers. The fact remained that there was no pursuance of matter with the beneficiaries
by the staff/authorities of health institutions.

8.1.3.5 Belated payment of incentive

Under JSY, disbursement of cash incentive was to be made to the beneficiaries immediately
after delivery or at the time of discharge. In six test checked units, payment of cash incentive
of % 3.01 crore to eligible beneficiaries was delayed for periods ranging from 8 to 780 days
(Appendix-8.4).

In the cases of PHC, Ashafpur (Budaun) and PHCs, Chittaura and Tejwapur (Bahraich), the
Government did not give any reply. However, findings of CHC, Mauranipur, Jhansi were
accepted. Non-availability and delayed release of fund were attributed as the reasons for
delayed payment in DH(F) Raebareli. In the case of CHC, Lakhauti, Bulandshahar, it was
stated that due to delayed submission of identity proof/Ration Card by the beneficiaries,
payment of incentive was not made on time. However, as per GO dated 08 June 2007, in case
of non-availability of any proof of identification with the beneficiary, MOIC was made
responsible to disburse the incentive to the beneficiary on any holiday in the presence of two
witnesses having independent identifications.

8.1.3.6 Payment of incentive to persons other than the beneficiary

Under NRHM framework, payment of incentive was to be made only to the beneficiary.
During 2006-07 to 2010-11, in three audited units हैं 105.17 lakh was paid to individuals other
than beneficiary, like husband, relatives, ANM and ASHA in 7912 cases of institutional
deliveries (Appendix-8.5).

8.1.5 Saubhagyavati Surakshit Matritva Yojna (SSMY)

Under the scheme launched in the State in May 2008, beneficiaries from BPL families were
to be provided delivery facilities at Private Nursing Homes, which were entitled to a payment
of € 1.85 lakh per hundred deliveries.

Audit observed in Unnao district that against an allotment of = 7.70 lakh under SSMY during
2008-11, only हें 3.72 lakh (48.31 per cent) was spent and the balance was lying unutilised,
resulting in blockage of fund.

In CMO, Bahraich, as per FMR, र 9.25 lakh was allotted for payment to Private Hospitals for
500 cases. It was observed that M/s Raj Hospital, Bahraich, empanelled under SSMY,
registered 258 cases of deliveries during 2008-09 to 2010-11, for which हैं 4.77 lakh at
र₹ 1850 per case was payable to them. But payment of ® 6.77 lakh was made to the hospital.

Audit observed that though the incentive was to be paid to beneficiaries by DHS itself,
payments of = 1.99 lakh to 140 rural and 3 urban beneficiaries were routed through M/s Raj
Hospital, retaining ~ 0.01 lakh. Further, photocopy of BPL cards for 258 beneficiaries was
not available in either the hospital or in DHS records. Non-possession of copy of BPL card
rendered the entire payment of % 6.77 lakh doubtful.

The Government accepted that due to ignorance of rules, incentive of % 1.99 lakh was paid to
beneficiaries by M/s Raj Hospital. The remaining amount हैं 0.01 lakh had been recovered.
(December 2011) from them. It further stated that photocopies of BPL cards were burnt in a
fire. However, in the report of Fire Fighting Department, no records were reported to have
been burnt in the fire. Only inverter, furniture and some pathological equipment/machines
were certified to be damaged in the fire.

Family Welfare, Reproductive and Child Health

al Family planning

The objective of the Mission was to reach the replacement levels of TFR of 2.1 by 2012. The
Family Planning Programme aimed to reduce the TFR by encouraging adoption of
appropriate family planning methods and increasing the Contraceptive Prevalence Rate. As
per PIP of 2011-12, TFR (2007-08) was 3.8. The Government stated that efforts were on to
achieve the goal and reasons for slow progress were also being evaluated through various
studies being conducted in the State.

8.2.1 Performance of various family planning methods

8.2.1.1 Limiting Methods

Limiting methods of family planning consist of vasectomy for male and tubectomy for
female. A target of 9.7 lakh cases per year was fixed for sterilisation in Uttar Pradesh, against
which shortfall ranged between 51 to 61 per cent. The status of target and achievement in the
State was as below:

Table 8.7: Achievements of sterilisation targets in the State

(in lakh)
2005-06 9.70 4.50 5.20 53.61
2006-07 9.70 4.29 5.41 55.77
2007-08 9.70 4.72 4.98 51.34
2008-09 9.70 4.80 4.90 50.52
2009-10 9.70 4.20 5.50 56.70
2010-11 9.70 3.79 5.91 60.93

(Source: Information collected during audit)

As would be evident from the above table, the achievement vis a vis target for sterlisation
declined from 49.48 in 2008-09 to 43.30 in 2009-10 and 39.07 per cent in
2010-11, although the targets remained constant during 2005-11.

No separate targets were fixed for vasectomy and tubectomy. Shortfall in achievement
against the target for sterilisation in the test checked districts ranged between 0 and
84 per cent. (Appendix-8.6)

The Government did not furnish any reply.

Vasectomy

With the aim of increasing participation of males in population stabilisation efforts, the Gol
developed a scheme to promote No-Scalpel Vasectomy (NSV). In the 22 test checked
districts while 11.46 lakh females underwent tubectomy operation during 2005-11, during the
same period, only 0.14 lakh males had under gone vasectomy operation. The ratio between
vasectomy and tubectomy was 1:82, signifying that implementation of limiting methods was
biased towards women. Vasectomy operations were only 0 to 5 per cent of total sterilisation
in the test checked districts. The average percentage of vasectomy for the period 2005-11

Performance Audit Report on National Rural Health Mission for the year ended 31 March 2011 | 143


Family Welfare, Reproductive and Child Health Welfare, Reproductive and Child Health

= between 0.04 (Etah) and two (Varanasi). No vasectomy was performed in Etah in
2008-09 and 2010-11. In Jalaun, no vasectomy was performed in 2005-06.

The Government did not furnish any reply.

Tubectomy

Tubectomy through laparoscopic procedure had the advantages of ease of operation and
quick recovery. 1293 laparoscopes, valuing 102.94 crore were procured during 2009-11.
This included 36 laparoscopes for training. To operate the above laparoscopes only
454 (36 per cent) trained doctors were available in the State as of March 2011.

The Government stated that the State had a pool of 411 trained doctors and it had trained
43 new doctors. No separate training was required to these doctors on the fresh stock of
laparoscopes. The reply was not acceptable as laparoscopes were more than two and half
times the number of trained doctors.

8.2.1.2 Spacing methods

Oral pills, condoms and Intra Uterine Device (IUD) insertions were the three prevailing
spacing methods of family planning to regulate fertility and promote couple protection rate.
A target of 26.11 lakh cases per year was fixed for IUD insertions in the State during
2005-11. The shortfall in IUD insertions in the State ranged from 24 per cent in 2005-06 to
42 per cent in 2009-10 (Appendix 8.7-A). The short fall in respect of IUD in the test checked
districts ranged between 22 and 48 per cent (Appendix-8.7-B). Among the spacing methods,
usage of condom constituted around 35 per cent, oral pills 18 per cent and IUD 47 per cent
during 2005-11. Decreasing trend of distribution of condoms and oral pills was observed
between 2006-07 and 2010-11. Also, in test checked districts distribution of condoms and
oral pills was declining.

The Government did not furnish any reply.

8.2.2 Quality Assurance

District Quality Assurance Committee (DQAC) was to be established in all the districts to
conduct provider-wise monitoring of sterilisation cases. These DQACs were to be actively
involved in verifying sterilisation cases as well as to ensure that standards and quality of
delivery of services were being followed. Besides, they had to submit monthly reports.
According to SHAP 2010-11, DQACs were established in all the districts.

Out of 22 test checked districts, status of DQAC was received in respect of 21 districts’.
Audit observed that in Allahabad, Bahraich, Ballia, Budaun, Deoria, Etah and Jhansi, DQACs
had not been formed till March 2011. In Jaunpur, DQAC was functioning for cases of failure
of sterilisation and deaths. In Bareilly, Bulandshahar, Jalaun, Kushi Nagar, Mirzapur,
Moradabad, Raebareli, and Shahjahanpur, DQAC had been reportedly constituted between
2005-06 and 2010-11 but no monitoring and verification of sterilisation cases were conducted
and no monthly reports were sent to the Directorate.

3 Allahabad, Azamgarh, Bahraich, Ballia, Bareilly, Budaun, Bulandshahar, Deoria, Etah, Gorakhpur, Jalaun, Jaunpur, Jhansi, Kanpur Nagar,

8.2.3, Cash compensation to beneficiaries of sterilisation

As per State Government orders compensation was payable to persons undergoing vasectomy
or tubectomy. SHS reported a total of 0.43 lakh cases of vasectomy and

worked out to % 181.57 crore (vasectomy: हैं 5.02 crore and tubectomy: हैं 176.55 crore).

. In CHC, Chunar, Mirzapur, payment of = 10.81 lakh was made to 1518 tubectomy

by signature/thumb impressions of the beneficiaries. The related forms which were
filled up by the motivator of beneficiaries did not mention the amount. Also, the thumb
impressions of the beneficiaries were taken on blank forms without mentioning the
amount to be paid to the beneficiaries resulting in doubtful payments of € 10.81 lakh.

. In District Hospital (Female), Gorakhpur, an expenditure of ~ 10.38 lakh was incurred

indicated that, against 198 cases of tubectomy, payment of % 0.09 lakh in 58 cases, to be

. In eight units* of test checked districts during 2005-11, payment of = 73.60 lakh for

inducers/ASHAs. There was nothing on record to show if inducers/ASHAs had paid the
received incentive to the actual beneficiaries.

the patients are discharged only after regaining consciousness and therefore, payment of

8.3 Routine Immunisation (RI

RI is an important child survival strategy, which mainly focuses on preventive aspects to
reduce morbidity to a great extent. The immunisation of children against six preventable
diseases, namely diphtheria, measles, pertussis, polio, tetanus and tuberculosis has been the
comerstone of RI under the Universal Immunisation Programme.

tabulated below:

For effective implementation of RI programme, availability of required quantities of vaccines

vaccines was gencrally lesser than the required quantity during 2005-11. Except Jaunpur, DT
vaccine was not available in the other districts during 2010-11.

The Government stated that (i) due to lesser community participation and utilisation of
private facilities, TT immunisation was low, (ii) Gol had promoted DPT in place of DT and
stopped supply of DT vaccine from 2010-11 and (iii) shortage of vaccine was due to irregular
supply by Gol. Reply indicated that either the targets set were unrealistic or steps taken for
immunisation of children were inadequate.

os campaign stipulated two National Immunisation Days (NIDs), six Special National

Immunisation Days (SNIDs) and additional rounds every 3-4 weeks in selected districts of

during 2005-11, the target of zero transmission by end of 2008 was not achieved. The number

of new polio cases in the State, however, reduced to 10 in 2010. In 22 test checked districts,

was reported after 21 April 2010.

8.4.2 Wastage of vaccines

The Central Store at District Headquarters receives polio vaccines from Gol, which are then
issued on the basis of requirements for RI and PPI. During movement, some vaccines become
unusable due to breakdown of cold chain and normal wastages. A wastage rate of 25 per cent,
or a Wastage Multiplication Factor (WME) of 1.33° is allowed for all vaccines supplied in
Uttar Pradesh. The WMF is used to arrive at the correct quantum of vaccine needed for an
immunisation session.

Test check of records revealed that in some test checked districts, the wastage was
considerably higher than the norms prescribed as tabulated below:

Table 8.11: Excess wastage of vaccines

The excess wastage of vaccines indicated poor cold chain management or pilferage. The loss
on account of excess wastage and missing stock in the three test checked districts, worked out
to % 3.62 crore. The detailed calculations are Appendix 8.8.

The Government accepted the excess wastage and stated that it was mainly due to handling of
vaccine by untrained personnel, kind of vaccine, instability of vaccine during summer,
administration of vaccine to children above five years and improper fitting of dropper on
vaccine.

9 National Disease Control Programmes

9.1 National Disease Control Programmes (NDCP)

Programmes for disease control such as National Programme for Control of Blindness,
National Vector Borne Disease Contro] Programme, Revised National Tuberculosis Control
Programme, National Leprosy Eradication Programme, National Iodine Deficiency Disorders
Control Programme and Integrated Disease Surveillance Project were brought under NRHM
umbrella. For planning, implementation and monitoring of these programmes, the
Government formed (16 November 2006) the “Medical and Health Programme
Implementation Committee” which was chaired by the Principal Secretary, Medical, Health
and Family Welfare. The Director General, Medical and Health was member secretary of this
committee and NDCPs were implemented by his Directorate under the supervision of SHM/
SHS.

9.1.1 National Programme for Control of Blindness (NPCB)

NPCB was launched in 1976 and brought into the ambit of NRHM in 2005-06. As per the
survey conducted in 2001-02, prevalence rate of blindness in Uttar Pradesh was one per cent.
NPCB aimed to reduce the cases of blindness to 0.5 per cent by 2012 through increased
cataract surgery (46 lakh cases by 2012); school eye screening and free distribution of
spectacles to children; and strengthening of infrastructure by way of supply of equipment and
imparting training to eye surgeons and nurses.

9.1.1.1 Cataract operations

Cataract operations were performed in the Government hospitals, private practitioners and by
NGOs. Sector wise details of cataract operations performed in the State during 2005-11 were
as under:

envisaged 600 per lakh population per year and ranged between 300 and 400 during 2005-11.

The Government stated that there were only 202 posts of Eye Surgeons in District Hospitals
and some selected hospitals. The number of operations performed by them was satisfactory.

9.1.1.2 Irregular payments to NGOs in eye care activities

Under NPCB, NGOs were engaged to carry out various eye care activities including
performance of free cataract operations. The scheme for participation of NGOs, relating to
cataract operation provides:

. NGO/Voluntary Organisation (VO), empanelled, should be registered under the Indian
Societies Registration Act, 1860/Charitable Public Trust registered under any extant
law and have facilities for secondary level eye care services, well trained staff,
infrastructure and the required managerial expertise to organise and carry out cataract
operation and other eye related activities; and

. Financial assistance upto a maximum of % 750! for performing each free cataract
operation.

Under the scheme, camps were allowed only for eye screening (not operation). The screened
patients were referred and all the cataract operations were performed in only Fixed OT
facility. CMOs of Agra, Budaun and Mirzapur accorded permission for holding eye screening
camps to NGOs from time to time. In these districts, NGOs performed cataract operations in
camps also and were irregularly paid ~ 66.98 lakh as below:

9.1.1.4 Inadmissible payment to NGOs

NPCB guidelines provided for a maximum payable amount of € 175 per cataract operation to
NGOs for identifying, motivating and transporting the beneficiaries to the Government/
NGOs fixed facilities.

Audit observed that:

e In March 2008, the Government Eye Surgeon performed 272 plain operations and
168 IOL operations in the District Hospital, Shahjahanpur. The DPM, Shahjahanpur
paid at the rate of हैं 450 and % 500 per plain and IOL operation respectively against
permissible rate of ¥ 175 per operations to two NGOs (Lions Club, Central: हैं 1.41 lakh
for 179 plain and 136 IOL operations, Lions Club Vishal: % 57,850 for 93 plain and
32 IOL operations) for identifying, motivating and transporting the beneficiaries to the
Government hospital resulting in excess payment of € 1.21 lakh.

The Government stated that payments were made as per the rates prescribed. Reply was
not correct as the operations were performed by the Government surgeon in the
Government hospital and as per NPCB guidelines, only = 175 per case was payable to
NGOs for identifying, motivating and transporting the beneficiaries to the Government
hospital.

. CMO, Allahabad recognised four to 10 NGOs for eye care activities and made

were based on the list of beneficiaries verified by the concerned MOICs. No records

these were stated to be available with DPM, DBCS. Due to above, the expenditure
incurred could not be verified.

The Government stated that beneficiaries were selected in screening camps and payments to
NGOs were made after due verification. Reply was not convincing as records relating to
holding of eye screening camps were not available with the concerned CHCs/PHCs.

9.2 National Iodine Deficiency Disorders Control Programme (NIDDCP)

NIDDCP was launched in 1992 to control Iodine Deficiency Disorders (IDDs). The
important objectives and components of NIDDCP are to - conduct surveys to assess the
magnitude of the IDDs; supply iodised salt in place of common salt through Public
Distribution System; conduct resurveys after every five years to assess the extent of IDDs and
the impact of iodised salt, monitor urinary iodine excretion and provide health education and
create awareness through publicity.

Out of 71 districts of the State, 54 districts were surveyed (1974-1992) in phases and
24 districts were identified as endemic to IDD. During 2005-2011, only eight districts were
surveyed (2005-07) and two re-surveyed (2006-07). During 2005-07, neither any target was
fixed nor was any salt sample analysed. During 2007-11, 1.80 lakh salt samples were tested.
Further, during 2008-11, 15.29 lakh tonnes of salt, against the allotment of 23.33 lakh tonnes,
were distributed. For public awareness, % five lakh was allotted under NIDDCP during
2007-08. No allocations were made for public awareness during 2008-11.

The Government accepted the facts and admitted that no funds were received from the
Government of India.

population by the end of 2012 and sustain the level thereof. The status of leprosy cases in the
